About the Author
Deepak Gowda is a distinguished engineering leader and hands-on
technology geek with over 20 years of experience, including a decade
focused on artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML).
He has a remarkable track record of driving transformation, having worked
with global conglomerates such as Bosch, Sony, Intel, Cisco, Verizon, and
Dell across various sectors, including supply chain, data center
infrastructure, data storage, and cybersecurity.
Known for his practical, hands-on approach, he has a remarkable track
record of driving innovation and transformation, with over 30 granted
patents in these fields.
His expertise includes optimizing GPU workloads on ultra-large compute
clusters to enable high-performance solutions.
Additionally, as an aviation enthusiast, he holds an FAA private pilot
license, reflecting his relentless curiosity, technical enthusiasm, passion for
exploration, and lifelong learning.

--- Strana 10 ---
Preface
The ever-evolving landscape of AI and ML has redefined how industries
function, from healthcare and finance to cybersecurity and autonomous
systems. This book is designed to serve as a comprehensive guide to
understanding and implementing deep learning models, data analytics, and
high-performance computing solutions.
This book aims to provide a hands-on, practical approach to AI and ML. It
highlights the importance of optimizing GPU workloads, leveraging
scalable compute clusters, and utilizing advanced architectures for real-
world applications. Through various chapters, we will explore key concepts
such as PyTorch setup, deep learning fundamentals, object detection,
generative adversarial networks, text-to-speech synthesis, and natural
language processing.
This book is structured to allow both beginners and experienced
professionals to gain insights into AI and ML with a focus on practical
implementation. Whether you are an AI researcher, a software engineer, or a
technology enthusiast, I hope this book equips you with the knowledge and
skills needed to drive innovation in the field of AI.
Chapter 1: A Primer on Deep Learning- Deep learning is a subset of ML
inspired by the human brain's neural networks. This chapter explores the
feasibility of early-stage deep learning, its rise due to hardware
advancements, and key components like neural networks, activation
functions, and optimization techniques. It discusses practical applications,
advantages, and challenges, including unsuitable scenarios and drawbacks.
The chapter also highlights datasets and algorithms that drive deep learning,
emphasizing how high-speed internet and computing power fuel its growth.

--- Strana 11 ---
Chapter 2: Getting PyTorch Setup and Running- This chapter introduces
PyTorch, a leading deep learning library, covering installation, tensors, data
handling, and model building. Readers will learn how to preprocess
datasets, use data loaders, and apply transformations. It covers PyTorch’s
automatic differentiation mechanism (Autograd) and the optimization loop,
which updates model parameters during training. The chapter concludes
with saving, loading, and using trained models for inference, ensuring a
smooth transition from model development to deployment.
Chapter 3: Multi-object Detection- This chapter focuses on object
detection, particularly classification-based multi-object detection using
convolutional neural networks (CNNs). It explains dataset preparation,
solution architecture, and model training with PyTorch. Readers will
understand bounding boxes, anchor boxes, and common object detection
algorithms like YOLO and Faster R-CNN. The chapter details evaluation
metrics, model optimization, and real-world applications in autonomous
driving, surveillance, and healthcare. A practical walkthrough on building
and testing object detection models is provided.
Chapter 4: Image Labeling Using Transfer Learning- Transfer learning
is a powerful technique in deep learning where pre-trained models are fine-
tuned for new tasks. This chapter explains the fundamentals of transfer
learning, its advantages, and practical applications in image labeling. It
explores feature extraction, model fine-tuning, and adapting architectures
like ResNet and VGG for new datasets. Readers will also learn about
transfer learning approaches such as domain adaptation, one-shot learning,
and zero-shot learning, along with optimization strategies.
Chapter 5: Harnessing Generative Adversarial Network for Data
Augmentation- GANs generate synthetic data resembling real-world
datasets. This chapter explains GAN fundamentals, architecture, training
mechanisms, and applications such as image generation, data augmentation,
and anomaly detection. It discusses GAN variants like deep convolutional
GANs (DCGANs) and their impact on medical imaging, fraud detection,
and artistic creation. Ethical concerns, performance evaluation, and real-

--- Strana 12 ---
world implementations are covered, with a step-by-step example for
implementing GANs in PyTorch.
Chapter 6: Building Text-to-speech Models - TTS technology converts
written text into human-like speech. This chapter explores the evolution of
TTS, its design, key algorithms (Tacotron, WaveNet, transformer-based
models), and dataset preprocessing. Readers will learn to train TTS models
using TorchAudio, understand spectrogram generation, and explore real-
world applications in accessibility, customer service, and voice assistants.
Ethical concerns, bias mitigation, and model selection strategies are also
discussed.
Chapter 7: Converting Speech-to-text Using TorchAudio- STT, or
automatic speech recognition (ASR), enables machines to transcribe
spoken words into text. This chapter covers audio preprocessing,
spectrogram generation, and deep learning models like Tacotron 2 and
WaveGlow. Applications include transcription services, accessibility tools,
and virtual assistants. Readers will learn about tokenization, phoneme
encoding, and practical implementations using TorchAudio. Challenges like
noise reduction, speaker independence, and multilingual support are
discussed.
Chapter 8: Text Analysis, Categorization, and Language Translation-
This chapter explores natural language processing (NLP) techniques for
text analysis, classification, and clustering. It covers preprocessing
techniques like tokenization, stop-word removal, and stemming, along with
word embeddings and deep learning-based classification. Readers will learn
how to build text classification models (binary, multi-class, and multi-
label), handle imbalanced datasets, and implement attention mechanisms.
Case studies highlight real-world applications in sentiment analysis, topic
modeling, and recommendation systems.
Chapter 9: Text Summarization and Sentiment Analysis- Text
summarization condenses lengthy documents while retaining key
information. 
This 
chapter 
discusses 
extractive 
and 
abstractive

--- Strana 13 ---
summarization, along with deep learning approaches like transformers.
Sentiment analysis evaluates emotional tone in text, crucial for social media
monitoring and customer feedback. Question answering systems retrieve
precise answers from text using models like BERT. Readers will explore
PyTorch implementations, real-world case studies, and best practices for
training and deploying these models.
Chapter 10: Time Series Forecasting Using Deep Learning- Time series
forecasting predicts future values based on past trends. This chapter
introduces traditional and deep learning-based approaches like RNNs,
LSTMs, CNNs, and transformers. It covers time series components (trend,
seasonality, cyclic behavior), anomaly detection, and demand forecasting.
Readers will learn model selection, architecture design, training techniques,
and hyperparameter tuning for time series prediction. Practical
implementations with PyTorch demonstrate forecasting in finance,
healthcare, and environmental science.

Chapter 1
A Primer on Deep Learning
Introduction
Deep learning is a subset of machine learning (ML) inspired by the human
brain’s structure and function. Deep learning algorithms can learn complex
data representations through a hierarchical approach. This allows them to
identify patterns and classify and recognize images, speech, text, and other
data types. It uses artificial neural networks (ANNs) with multiple layers
to learn and extract features from data.
Structure
This chapter covers the following topics:
Feasibility of early-stage deep learning
The rise of deep learning
Deep learning revolution
High-speed internet is the fuel for deep learning
Advantages of deep learning
Components of deep learning

--- Strana 27 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Mechanics of deep learning
Activation functions in deep learning
Deep learning model optimization
Practical applications of deep learning
Hardware for deep learning
Datasets for deep learning
Algorithms for deep learning
Scenarios not suitable for deep learning
Drawbacks of deep learning
Objectives
In this chapter, the objectives are to explore the historical challenges and
subsequent rise of deep learning, understand its core advantages, and
examine its fundamental components. We will delve into the intricacies of
the deep learning process, from problem definition to deployment, and
investigate its practical applications across various sectors. Additionally, the
chapter aims to highlight the critical developments in datasets, algorithms,
and hardware that have propelled the field forward. Finally, we will address
potential drawbacks and unsuitable applications, setting the stage for a
comprehensive understanding of deep learning's transformative impact on
technology and industry.
Feasibility of early-stage deep learning
Deep learning was not popular earlier due to the following reasons:
Lack of computational power: In the past, computers did not have
the computational power to train deep neural networks with many
layers.
Lack of data: Deep learning algorithms require large amounts of
labeled data to train their models effectively. In the past, datasets

--- Strana 28 ---
•
•
1.
2.
were often small, making it challenging to train deep learning
models.
Lack of understanding of deep learning algorithm and research:
The concept of deep learning has existed since the 1980s, but the
field did not gain significant attention until the early 2010s.
Researchers made breakthroughs in developing efficient algorithms
for training deep neural networks and demonstrated their
effectiveness in various applications.
Lack of practical applications: In the past, there were fewer
practical applications for deep learning, and simpler ML algorithms
were sufficient for most tasks. However, the demand for deep
learning has increased with the growth of big data, smartphones, and
other data-centric applications.
The rise of deep learning
Deep learning is now popular for a few reasons:
Birth of cloud computing and elasticity of computational
hardware: Today,
thanks to the advancement in cloud computing technologies, almost
anyone anywhere in the world can access highly sophisticated
hardware with varying computational power at the click of a button.
Cloud computing has made access to computing, storage, and
networking affordable to everyone, previously limited to only large
enterprises.
Digitization of enterprise business processes and explosion of
devices: Large enterprises have transformed their business from
manual to digital using sophisticated software. This has led to
significant growth of enterprise data. Before 2000, most people
primarily used personal computers. Later, due to the rapid
popularity of smartphones, data creation multiplied exponentially.
This led to massive data growth in volume, velocity, and variety.

--- Strana 29 ---
3.
•
•
•
•
Traditional software/applications could no longer scale to handle the
data. Availability of large amounts of data and varieties of data
(image, sound, text, logs, etc.) needed sophisticated algorithms and
applications.
Expansion of 4G and high-speed internet networks and
explosion of practical applications: The vast expansion of data
footprint due to cellular 4G and high-speed internet networks
worldwide made many devices previously not connected to the data
network enable streaming of the data. This led to more than ten
billion Internet of Things (IoT) devices.
With robust hardware with high computational power and large datasets
available, deep learning has become more accessible, and its popularity has
grown rapidly. As a result, deep learning is now widely used.
Deep learning revolution
Deep learning has evolved through milestones and breakthroughs over the
past few decades. Here are some of the key developments in the history of
deep learning:
Development of ANNs: The concept of ANNs was first introduced
in the 1950s. However, significant progress was made only after
1980 in developing efficient algorithms for training ANNs.
Backpropagation algorithm: In 1986, the backpropagation
algorithm was designed and conceived to train deep neural networks
with many layers efficiently.
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs): In the 1990s, CNNs were
developed to recognize objects in images efficiently and accurately.
Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBMs): In 2006, RBMs were
introduced, enabling deep neural networks to learn data
representations without explicit labels using unsupervised learning.

--- Strana 30 ---
•
•
•
•
•
Deep belief networks (DBNs): In 2006, DBNs were introduced.
Combined with RBMs, this supervised learning achieved state-of-
the-art results in various tasks, such as image and speech
recognition.
Deep learning breakthroughs: In the early 2010s, deep learning
experienced a breakthrough in performance, fueled by the
availability of large datasets, powerful GPUs, and improved
algorithms. This led to significant improvements in various
applications, such as speech recognition, natural language
processing (NLP), and computer vision.
Advancements in architectures: After 2010, various architectures
have been developed to improve the performance of deep learning,
including recurrent neural networks (RNNs), transformers, and
generative adversarial networks (GANs).
Overall, the evolution of deep learning has been driven by advances in
hardware, software, and algorithms, the availability of large datasets, and
the growth of the internet.
High-speed internet is the fuel for deep learning
High-speed internet can expand deep learning in several ways:
Faster data transfer: High-speed internet networks offer faster data
transfer rates, enabling more significant amounts of data to be
transferred quickly. This is especially important for deep learning,
which relies on large data for training.
Lower latency: High-speed internet networks offer lower latency
than previous generations of networks, which reduces the delay
between data transfer and response. This is important for real-time
applications such as autonomous driving, where fast response times
are crucial.

--- Strana 31 ---
•
•
•
•
•
Edge computing: High-speed internet networks enable edge
computing, which involves processing data closer to the source
instead of sending it to a centralized cloud server. This reduces deep
learning applications’ latency and bandwidth requirements, making
them more efficient.
Improved connectivity: High-speed internet networks offer
improved connectivity, which is essential for deploying IoT devices
that generate large amounts of data. This can help to improve the
accuracy and efficiency of deep learning applications.
Increased scalability: High-speed internet networks can support a
larger number of connected devices, which can help to scale up deep
learning applications. This can be especially important for
applications such as smart cities, where many devices must be
connected and managed.
Overall, high-speed internet networks can expand the capabilities of deep
learning by enabling faster and more efficient data transfer, reducing
latency, enabling edge computing, improving connectivity, and increasing
scalability.
Advantages of deep learning
Deep learning has gained popularity in recent years due to its ability to
learn complex patterns in large datasets, leading to better performance in
various tasks such as image recognition, NLP, speech recognition, and
autonomous driving. Some advantages of deep learning include the
following:
Feature learning: Deep learning algorithms can automatically learn
the relevant features of a dataset, eliminating the need for manual
feature extraction.
Scalability: Deep learning models can handle large amounts of data,
making them suitable for big data applications.

--- Strana 32 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
Adaptability: Deep learning models can adapt to new data and
situations, making them suitable for various applications.
Accuracy: Deep learning models have achieved state-of-the-art
results in various tasks, such as image recognition, NLP, and speech
recognition, often surpassing human-level performance.
Automation: Deep learning models can automate many tasks,
reducing the need for human intervention and leading to increased
efficiency and productivity.
Due to these advantages, deep learning has become popular for solving
complex problems in various fields, such as healthcare, finance, and
robotics.
Components of deep learning
Following are the main components of deep learning:
Neural networks: A neural network consists of connected neurons
organized into layers. Each neuron receives one or more inputs,
applies a set of weights and biases to those inputs, and passes the
result through an activation function to produce an output. Neural
networks can have many layers and can learn complex patterns in
data.
Training data: Deep learning models require large amounts of
labeled training data to learn from. The training data is used to
adjust the weights and biases of the neural network to make accurate
predictions on new, unseen data.
Loss function: The loss function measures how well the neural
network performs on the training data. It compares the predicted
output of the network to the true output and computes a numerical
value representing the error or loss. The goal of training the network
is to minimize the loss function by adjusting the weights and biases
of the network.

--- Strana 33 ---
•
•
•
Optimizer: The optimizer is an algorithm used to adjust the weights
and biases of the neural network during training to minimize the loss
function. Popular optimizers include stochastic gradient descent
(SGD), Adam, and adaptive gradient (AdaGrad).
Validation data: Besides the training data, a separate set of
validation data monitors the network’s performance during training
and is used to select the best model. The validation data is not used
to adjust the weights and biases of the network but is instead used to
evaluate how well the network is generalizing to new, unseen data.
Test data: Once the network has been trained, a separate set of test
data is used to evaluate its performance on new, unseen data. The
test data is not used during training or validation but to measure how
well the network will perform in the real-world.
The diagram below provides visualization:
Figure 1.1: Neural network components
Overall, deep learning is a powerful approach to learning from complex
data and has been successfully applied to a wide range of tasks, including
image recognition, NLP, and speech recognition. However, it requires large
amounts of labeled training data and can be computationally expensive to
train and deploy.
Mechanics of deep learning

--- Strana 34 ---
1.
2.
3.
Deep learning works by training ANNs with several layers to learn hidden
representations of data used to make predictions or classifications. Here are
the general steps involved in deep learning:
Deep learning, a subset of ML, has revolutionized fields such as computer
vision, NLP, and predictive analytics. This transformative technology
mimics the workings of the human brain in processing data and creating
patterns for decision-making. Here, we discuss the intricacies of the deep
learning process, covering its foundational steps in detail.
Figure 1.2: Deep learning process flow
Problem definition: The journey begins with a clear articulation of
the problem at hand. This stage involves understanding the task,
whether it is recognizing objects in images, translating languages, or
forecasting future trends. A well-defined problem statement guides
the choice of data, model architecture, and evaluation metrics,
setting a clear direction for the project.
Data collection: Data is the lifeblood of deep learning. The
collection phase involves gathering a large and diverse dataset that
accurately represents the problem space. This dataset should
encompass the various inputs the model is expected to handle,
ensuring it learns a comprehensive representation of the task. The
quality, diversity, and relevance of the collected data significantly
impact the model's ability to learn and generalize.
Data preprocessing: Raw data often contains noise, irrelevant
information, and inconsistencies that can hinder learning.

--- Strana 35 ---
4.
5.
6.
7.
Preprocessing transforms this raw data into a clean, standardized
format suitable for training. This step can include normalization
(scaling input values), handling missing values, encoding
categorical data, and augmenting the dataset to increase its diversity
and size. For image tasks, preprocessing might involve resizing
images, adjusting brightness, or cropping to focus on relevant
regions.
Model selection: Selecting an appropriate model architecture is
critical. The choice depends on the nature of the problem: CNNs
excel in image-related tasks, RNNs are suited for sequential data
like time series or text, and transformers have shown remarkable
success in language understanding. The model architecture defines
the blueprint of layers and connections that will learn from the data.
Model configuration: Once the architecture is selected, it needs to
be configured with specific parameters. This includes defining the
number and type of layers, the activation functions that introduce
non-linearities, and the initialization of weights. Additionally, the
choice of loss function, which quantifies the difference between the
model's predictions and the actual data, and the optimization
algorithm, which updates the model's weights, are crucial
configurations that influence learning efficiency and outcome.
Training: Training is the core phase where the model learns from
the data. Using the training set, the model iteratively makes
predictions, compares them against the actual outcomes, and adjusts
its weights to minimize the loss function. This process of forward
propagation 
(making 
predictions), 
calculating 
loss, 
and
backpropagation (updating weights) continues for multiple epochs
until the model adequately learns the data patterns.
Evaluation: Evaluating the model's performance is essential to
understand its efficacy. Using a test set representing real-world data,
the model's predictions are compared against true values using

--- Strana 36 ---
8.
9.
10.
11.
metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score, depending
on the nature of the task. This evaluation helps identify overfitting,
where the model performs well on training data but poorly on
unseen data, indicating it has not learned generalizable patterns.
Hyperparameter tuning: Hyperparameters, such as learning rate,
batch size, and number of epochs, play a significant role in model
training and performance. Tuning these parameters involves
experimenting with different values to find the combination that
yields the best performance. Techniques like grid search, random
search, or more advanced methods like Bayesian optimization can
automate this search, although it can be computationally intensive.
Model fine-tuning: Fine-tuning involves making final adjustments
to improve model performance. Techniques such as transfer
learning, where a model pre-trained on a large dataset is adapted to a
specific task, can significantly boost performance, especially when
data is scarce. This step might also involve additional training
rounds or adjusting model components to better capture the nuances
of the task.
Deployment: A well-trained model needs to be deployed into a
production environment to start delivering value. Deployment also
often includes model quantization, scaling weights, and activations
from float32 to int8. This involves integrating the model into
existing systems or applications where it can receive new inputs and
provide predictions or insights. Deployment also requires
considering scalability, latency, and reliability to ensure the model
performs well under real-world conditions.
Monitoring and maintenance: Post-deployment, continuous
monitoring is crucial to ensure the model remains effective over
time. Real-world data can change, leading to a phenomenon called
model drift, where the model's performance degrades. Regular

--- Strana 37 ---
•
•
evaluation against fresh data, updating the dataset, and retraining the
model are necessary steps to maintain its relevance and accuracy.
In conclusion, the deep learning process is a meticulous journey from
defining the problem to deploying a robust model. Each step, from data
collection and preprocessing to training, evaluation, and fine-tuning, is
critical in shaping the model's ability to learn and make accurate predictions
by rigorously following these steps and continuously monitoring and
updating the model.
Neuron basics
A neuron is the basic building block of a neural network. It receives one or
more inputs, processes them using a set of weights and biases, and produces
an output.
Figure 1.3: Basics of a neuron
Here is a more detailed explanation of how a neuron works:
Inputs: A neuron receives one or more inputs, which may be raw
data or outputs from other neurons in the network.
Weights: Each input is assigned a weight, determining its influence
on the neuron's output. The weights are learned during training and
adjusted to optimize the network’s performance.

--- Strana 38 ---
•
•
•
Bias: Besides the inputs and weights, a neuron also has a bias term,
a constant value added to the weighted sum of the inputs. The bias
allows the neuron to shift its activation function to the left or right,
which can help model complex data.
Activation function: Once the inputs, weights, and biases have
been combined, the neuron applies an activation function to the
result. The activation function determines whether the neuron will
fire based on its received inputs.
Output: The neuron output results from the activation function.
This output may be passed on to other neurons in the network as an
input or the network’s final output.
During the training process, the weights and biases of the neuron are
adjusted to optimize the network’s performance. This is typically done
using an optimization algorithm such as SGD, which iteratively updates the
weights and biases to minimize the difference between the predicted output
and the true output.
The activation function used by the neuron is a crucial component of its
behavior. Common activation functions include the sigmoid, hyperbolic
tangent (tanh), and Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU) functions. The choice
of activation function can significantly impact the network’s performance,
and different functions may be more suited to different data types.
Neurons are robust, flexible building blocks that enable neural networks to
learn patterns and relationships in complex data. Neurons can be combined
to form a multi-layered neural network. By adjusting the weights and biases
of the neurons, neural networks can adapt to a wide range of tasks and data
types. Refer to the following figure:

--- Strana 39 ---
•
Figure 1.4: Neural network
Activation functions in deep learning
Activation functions are an essential component of deep learning models.
They are applied to the output of a neuron to determine whether it should
fire or not based on the input it has received.
Here are some standard activation functions used in deep learning:
Sigmoid: The sigmoid function is a popular activation function used
in the early days of neural networks. It takes an input and squashes
it to a value between 0 and 1, which can be interpreted as a
probability. However, sigmoid functions can suffer from the
vanishing gradient problem, which can slow down or prevent
training in deep networks.

--- Strana 40 ---
•
•
Figure 1.5: Sigmoid function curve
tanh: The tanh function is similar to the sigmoid function but maps
the input to a range between -1 and 1. Like the sigmoid function,
tanh can also suffer from the vanishing gradient problem.
Figure 1.6: tanh function curve
ReLU: The ReLU function is a popular activation function in deep
learning. It takes an input and returns either the input value or 0,
whichever is larger. ReLU functions are computationally efficient
and can accelerate training in deep networks. However, ReLU
functions can also suffer from the dying ReLU problem, where the
gradient becomes zero for some input values, preventing the neuron
from learning.

--- Strana 41 ---
•
•
Figure 1.7: ReLU function curve
Leaky ReLU: The leaky ReLU function is a modified version of the
ReLU function that addresses the dying ReLU problem. It
introduces a slight slope for negative input values, allowing the
neuron to continue learning even when the gradient is zero.
Figure 1.8: Leaky ReLU function curve
Softmax: The softmax function is commonly used in the output
layer of a neural network for multiclass classification problems. It
takes an input and squashes it to a probability distribution over the
possible classes.

--- Strana 42 ---
•
•
Figure 1.9: Softmax function curve
Choosing the proper activation function for a deep learning model is an
important task, as it can significantly impact the model's performance. The
choice may depend on the data type, the task at hand, and the model's
architecture.
Deep learning model optimization
Optimizing deep learning models is a multifaceted process that involves
careful consideration of the model architecture, the data, and the training
process. Here is a comprehensive approach to optimization:
Data quality and augmentation: Quality data is fundamental for
training effective deep learning models. Ensure your data is clean,
well-labeled, and representative of the problem you want to solve.
Data augmentation can artificially expand the dataset through
transformations, adding variety and reducing overfitting.
Model architecture: Choose the right architecture for the task—
CNNs for image-related tasks, RNNs for sequential data, etc.
Simplify the model to the minimum complexity that achieves
desired performance to improve training speed and reduce
overfitting.

--- Strana 43 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Regularization techniques: Apply regularization methods like L1
and L2 regularization to penalize large weights and dropouts to
prevent co-adaptation of neurons. These techniques encourage
simpler models that generalize better to new data.
Batch 
normalization: 
Incorporate 
batch 
normalization 
to
standardize the inputs to a layer for each mini-batch. This stabilizes
the learning process and dramatically reduces the number of training
epochs required to train deep networks.
Learning rate and optimizers: Optimize the learning rate, as it
determines the size of the steps the algorithm takes during
optimization. Use adaptive learning rate optimizers like Adam,
RMSprop, or AdaGrad which can help converge faster than
traditional SGD.
Gradient clipping: Implement gradient clipping to prevent the
exploding gradient problem by capping the gradients during
backpropagation to a defined range.
Weight initialization: Properly initialize weights using strategies
like Xavier/Glorot or He initialization based on the activation
function to prevent vanishing or exploding gradients early in
training.
Hyperparameter tuning: Use grid search, random search, or
Bayesian optimization for hyperparameter tuning to find the optimal
settings for your model.
Early stopping: Monitor performance on a validation set and stop
training when performance deteriorates or ceases to improve, which
indicates that the model has begun to overfit.
Transfer learning: When data is limited, consider using transfer
learning where a model pretrained on a similar task is fine-tuned on
the specific dataset. This can lead to significant performance gains
and reduced training time.

--- Strana 44 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Ensemble methods: Combine the predictions from multiple
models. Ensembles often yield better performance than any single
model, as they can average out errors and reduce variance.
Evaluation metrics: Use the right evaluation metrics for your
specific problem, like accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score for
classification tasks, or mean squared error for regression tasks.
By carefully adjusting these factors, you can fine-tune your deep learning
model to improve its performance, speed up training, and achieve better
generalization to new, unseen data.
Practical applications of deep learning
Deep learning can be used for various tasks, including:
Computer vision: Deep learning can be used for object detection,
image segmentation, and facial recognition.
NLP: Deep learning can be used for tasks such as language
translation, sentiment analysis, speech recognition, and text
generation.
Recommender systems: Deep learning can be used to build
recommender systems to suggest products, services, etc.
Anomaly detection: Deep learning can detect anomalies and fraud
in financial transactions, credit card usage, or insurance claims.
Autonomous driving: Deep learning can be used to build
autonomous driving systems.
Robotics: Deep learning can be used to build robots performing
tasks such as gripping, manipulation, and navigation in industrial
environments.
Healthcare: Deep learning can analyze medical images, diagnose
diseases, and predict patient outcomes.
The diagram below captures some of the applications of deep learning:

--- Strana 45 ---
•
•
Figure 1.10: Deep learning applications
These are just a few examples of what can be done with deep learning.
Deep learning applications are diverse and continue to expand as the field
advances.
Hardware for deep learning
The hardware evolution for deep learning has been critical for its success as
deep learning models are computationally intensive and require high-
performance and distributed computing systems. Some key milestones are
as follows:
CPUs: CPUs were the first hardware used for deep learning. They
were initially used for training small neural networks and could not
be used for training more prominent models.
Graphics processing units (GPUs): GPUs were more efficient than
CPUs for deep learning. They were first used in 2007 for deep
learning applications and quickly gained popularity due to their
ability to process large amounts of data in parallel.

--- Strana 46 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
Tensor Processing Units (TPUs): They were developed by Google
in 2015 to accelerate deep learning applications. They are designed
explicitly for matrix operations, essential for deep learning
computations.
Field programmable gate arrays (FPGAs): They have been used
for deep learning since the early 2010s. They are programmable
integrated circuits that can be customized for specific deep learning
applications, making them flexible and efficient solutions.
Application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs): They have been
developed for deep learning to provide even more efficient and
specialized hardware. ASICs are designed for specific deep learning
applications, such as image recognition or speech processing, and
are optimized for performance and power consumption.
Quantum computing: Quantum computing has the potential to
significantly accelerate deep learning applications by leveraging
quantum 
algorithms 
to 
process 
large 
amounts 
of 
data
simultaneously. However, quantum computing for deep learning is
still in its early-stages of development.
Datasets for deep learning
The evolution of datasets for deep learning has been crucial for the success
of the field, as deep learning models require large amounts of labeled data
for training. Here are some key milestones in the dataset evolution for deep
learning:
MNIST: The MNIST dataset is one of the most well-known datasets
in deep learning. It consists of 60,000 images of handwritten digits
for training and 10,000 images for testing and has been used to
benchmark various deep learning algorithms.
ImageNet: The ImageNet dataset, introduced in 2009, consists of
over 14 million labeled images in over 20,000 categories. It has

--- Strana 47 ---
•
•
•
•
been used in various competitions and benchmarks and has helped
drive computer vision advances.
Common Objects in Context (COCO): The COCO dataset,
introduced in 2014, is a large-scale dataset for object detection,
segmentation, and captioning. It comprises over 330,000 images
with over 2.5 million object instances labeled across 80 categories.
OpenAI generative pre-trained transformer (GPT): The OpenAI
GPT dataset, introduced in 2018, consists of over 40GB of text data
from the internet. It has been used to train language models that
generate coherent, context-aware text.
Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers
(BERT): The BERT dataset, introduced in 2018, is a large-scale
dataset for NLP tasks such as question answering and sentiment
analysis. It has been used to train language models to understand the
context and meaning of words in sentences.
TIMIT: The TIMIT dataset, introduced in 1986, is a well-known
dataset for speech recognition. It consists of recordings of 630
speakers from eight significant dialects of American English.
Overall, the evolution of datasets for deep learning has been critical for the
field's success, enabling the development of more accurate and efficient
models. The trend towards larger and more diverse datasets will continue as
deep learning models become more complex and require more training data
to achieve higher accuracy. Additionally, the development of transfer
learning and data augmentation techniques have enabled smaller datasets,
making it easier to train deep learning models in various applications.
Algorithms for deep learning
Deep learning algorithms are a subset of ML algorithms based on ANNs.
Here are some of the most used deep learning algorithms:

--- Strana 48 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
CNNs: CNNs are primarily used for image recognition and
classification tasks. They are designed to identify image patterns and
can be used for object recognition, face detection, and image
segmentation tasks.
RNNs: RNNs are used for tasks involving sequential data, such as
speech recognition, language translation, and sentiment analysis.
They are designed to process data with a temporal component, such
as speech or text.
Long-short term memory (LSTM): LSTMs are a type of RNN
designed to handle long term dependencies. They are commonly
used for NLP tasks, where they can be used to generate text or
predict the next word in a sentence.
GANs: GANs are used for tasks involving generative modeling,
such as image and video synthesis. They consist of two neural
networks: A generator and a discriminator, which work together to
generate new data similar to the training data.
Autoencoders: Autoencoders are used for data compression, image
denoising, and anomaly detection tasks. They are designed to learn a
compressed representation of the input data, which can be used to
reconstruct the original data.
DBNs: DBNs are a type of neural network used for unsupervised
learning tasks such as feature learning and data clustering. They
consist of multiple layers of neurons and can learn to extract
complex features from the input data.
Transformer networks: This utilizes self-attention mechanisms to
handle sequential data efficiently, enabling parallel processing. They
have revolutionized NLP, powering tasks like translation, text
generation, and even vision, significantly improving model accuracy
and scalability.

--- Strana 49 ---
•
•
•
•
Overall, the choice of deep learning algorithm depends on the specific task
and the data type used. Different algorithms have different strengths and
weaknesses, and choosing the right algorithm is important in building an
effective deep learning system.
Scenarios not suitable for deep learning
While deep learning is a powerful and versatile technology, there are some
areas where it may not be the best approach or may not be applicable. Here
are a few examples:
Small data sets: Deep learning requires large amounts of data to be
effective and may not perform well on tasks where only a small
amount of data is available. Deep learning methods such as few-shot
learning may overcome this challenge.
Highly structured data: Deep learning is best suited for tasks
where the data has some underlying structure or pattern to be
learned. It may not perform well on tasks where the data is highly
structured, such as in database management or data cleaning.
Real-time 
processing: 
Deep 
learning 
algorithms 
can 
be
computationally intensive and may not be suitable for applications
that require real-time processing or low-latency response times.
Domain-specific knowledge: Deep learning algorithms may not be
practical when domain-specific knowledge is required to solve a
problem. For example, a deep learning algorithm may not be able to
diagnose a specific medical condition without the input of a medical
expert.
In general, it is essential to carefully consider the strengths and limitations
of deep learning when choosing an approach for a given problem. While it
is a powerful tool, it is not always the best choice for every situation.
Drawbacks of deep learning

--- Strana 50 ---
•
•
•
•
•
While deep learning has been successful in a wide range of applications,
there have also been some notable failures and limitations. Here are a few
examples:
Data bias: Deep learning models can be susceptible to bias in the
training data, leading to biased or unfair predictions. This has been
observed in various applications, including facial recognition
systems. The models have been shown to perform poorly on specific
groups of people, particularly those with darker skin tones.
Generalization issues: Deep learning models can sometimes
struggle to generalize to new and unseen data, mainly if the training
data is not representative of the target population or the data
distribution changes over time.
Explainability and interpretability: Deep learning models can be
challenging to interpret and explain, which can be problematic when
transparency and interpretability are essential. This can lead to
challenges in deploying these models in specific domains, such as
healthcare and finance.
Adversarial attacks: Deep learning models can be vulnerable to
adversarial attacks, where small changes to the input data can cause
the model to make incorrect predictions. This has been demonstrated
in 
various 
applications, 
including 
image 
recognition 
and
autonomous vehicles.
Resource requirements: Deep learning models can require
significant computational resources, including large amounts of
memory and processing power. This makes them difficult to deploy
on resource-constrained devices or in real-time applications.
It is essential to carefully consider the potential limitations and failures of
deep learning when developing and deploying models. It may be necessary
to incorporate additional techniques, such as interpretability methods and

--- Strana 51 ---
bias mitigation strategies, to address these challenges and ensure that the
models are fair, reliable, and trustworthy.
Conclusion
The evolution of deep learning has been a remarkable journey in the field of
artificial intelligence (AI). Over the past few decades, deep learning has
grown from a niche field to a mainstream technology that powers many of
the AI applications we use today. The rapid evolution of deep learning has
been driven by advances in hardware, algorithms, and data availability.
In the early days of deep learning, researchers struggled to train deep neural
networks with many layers. However, breakthroughs such as the
development of backpropagation algorithms and CNNs paved the way for
the development of more sophisticated deep learning architectures.
The availability of large datasets and the rise of cloud computing further
fueled the growth of deep learning. The development of powerful GPUs and
specialized hardware, such as Google TPUs, allowed for the training of
much larger and more complex models.
Today, deep learning is widely used in many fields, from computer vision
and NLP to healthcare and finance. Researchers continue to push the
boundaries of deep learning, exploring new architectures, algorithms, and
applications.
In conclusion, the evolution of deep learning has been a remarkable journey
that has transformed the field of AI. The technology has come a long way
since its inception, and it is poised to continue making significant
contributions to many fields in the years to come.
In the next chapter, we will discuss how to set up the PyTorch to train using
deep learning.
Points to remember

Deep learning is a branch of machine learning that model’s high-
level patterns in data as complex multilayered networks.
It draws inspiration from the human brain, utilizing structures
known as ANNs.
Initial challenges in deep learning included computational
limitations, scarce data, and a limited understanding of efficient
algorithms.
Its rise was propelled by advancements in computational power and
data availability.
Deep learning excels in feature learning, scalability, adaptability,
and achieving high accuracy, often surpassing human-level
performance in specific tasks.
Neural networks, training data, loss functions, optimizers, validation
data, and test data are fundamental to deep learning frameworks.
The deep learning process involves problem definition, data
collection and preprocessing, model selection and configuration,
training, evaluation, and fine-tuning.
Deep learning has been successfully applied across various domains
such as computer vision, NLP, autonomous driving, and more.
Milestones include the creation of pivotal datasets like ImageNet,
advancements in network architectures like CNNs and RNNs, and
the advent of hardware capable of supporting intensive
computations.
While powerful, deep learning faces issues such as data bias,
generalization, explainability, and vulnerability to adversarial
attacks.
Effective model optimization involves data augmentation, careful
selection of architecture and activation functions, regularization
techniques, and hyperparameter tuning. The field is evolving, with

--- Strana 53 ---
ongoing research aimed at addressing current limitations and
exploring new applications and methodologies.

Introduction
In this chapter, we embark on a comprehensive journey through the
essential components of PyTorch, a leading deep learning library that has
gained immense popularity for its flexibility, ease of use, and dynamic
computation graph. Our exploration will commence with the foundational
step of setting up PyTorch in your development environment, ensuring you
have the right tools to begin crafting sophisticated deep learning models.
You will be guided through the installation process of PyTorch. This section
will provide a step-by-step guide on how to set up PyTorch on various
operating systems, including essential troubleshooting tips to ensure a
smooth installation process. Whether you are working on Windows,
macOS, or Linux, you will be equipped with the knowledge to get PyTorch
up and running on your machine.
At the heart of PyTorch are tensors, the backbone of any deep learning
model. This segment will delve into the intricacies of tensors, their
similarities and differences from NumPy arrays, and their critical role in
representing data and computational graphs in PyTorch. You will learn how

--- Strana 55 ---
to manipulate tensors to perform a wide array of mathematical operations
that are fundamental to building neural networks.
Data is the lifeblood of any machine learning (ML) model. In this section,
we will explore how PyTorch handles datasets through its comprehensive
APIs. You will learn how to efficiently manage and preprocess large
datasets using data loaders and datasets, enabling seamless integration and
manipulation of data for training your models. This knowledge is pivotal in
ensuring your models are fed with well-organized and efficiently batched
data.
Data rarely comes in a clean, model-ready format. The Transforms section
will teach you how to use PyTorch's powerful transformation utilities to
preprocess and augment data, making it suitable for neural network
training. From normalizing to resizing and flipping images, you will master
the art of preparing your data for optimal model performance.
With the foundations in-place, we will shift our focus to constructing deep
learning models in PyTorch. This segment will guide you through the
process of building a model, layer by layer, using PyTorch's modular
approach. You will gain hands-on experience in defining custom neural
network architectures tailored to your specific problem domain.
One of PyTorch's most compelling features is its automatic differentiation
engine, autograd. This section will demystify the concept of automatic
differentiation and illustrate how PyTorch simplifies the computation of
gradients, which are essential for updating model parameters during
training. Understanding autograd is crucial for anyone looking to grasp the
inner workings of training deep learning models.
The heart of model training lies in the optimization loop, where the magic
of learning happens. This part of the chapter will walk you through the
iterative process of forward propagation, loss computation, backward
propagation, and parameter updates. You will learn how to use PyTorch's
optimizers to minimize loss and improve your model's accuracy over time.

--- Strana 56 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Finally, a model is only as good as its applicability to real-world problems.
This concluding section will cover the critical steps of saving and loading
trained models in PyTorch, allowing you to preserve your model's state for
future use or deployment. You will also learn how to utilize your trained
model for making predictions, bringing your deep learning projects to
fruition.
By the end of this chapter, you will have a solid understanding of PyTorch's
core functionalities and be well-equipped to tackle a wide range of deep
learning challenges. Let us embark on this exciting journey together and
unlock the full potential of PyTorch in your deep learning endeavors.

PyTorch installation and setup
Installing PyTorch on a Linux system is a straightforward process. The
installation steps can vary slightly depending on whether you are using a
CPU or a graphical processing unit (GPU) for computation. Below are the
general steps to install PyTorch on Linux:
Check Python installation: Ensure Python is installed on your
system. PyTorch requires Python, and it is recommended to use
Python 3.6 or newer. You can check your Python version by running
the following command in your terminal:
Python3 –version
If Python is not installed, you can install it using your distribution's
package manager, for example, apt for Ubuntu/Debian or yum for
Fedora.
Install pip (If necessary): pip is the Python package installer. If it
is not already installed, you can install it by running:
sudo apt install python3-pip # For Debian/Ubuntu
# or
sudo yum install python3-pip # For Fedora
Choose your installation preferences: Decide whether you will be
using PyTorch with only CPU support or with GPU support. For
GPU support, ensure that you have the necessary NVIDIA drivers,
CUDA Toolkit, and cuDNN installed. The CUDA version will affect
the PyTorch version you can install.

--- Strana 58 ---
4.
•
•
•
•
5.
Get the PyTorch install command: Visit the PyTorch official
website 
(https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/) 
to 
get 
the
appropriate install command for your setup. The website provides a
command based on:
Your OS (select Linux)
Package manager (pip or conda)
Python version
Whether you need CUDA support (select the CUDA
version compatible with your GPU drivers or none for
CPU-only)
For a typical CPU-only installation using pip, the command might
look like this:
pip3 install torch torchvision torchaudio
For a GPU-enabled installation, assuming you have the compatible
CUDA version installed, the command might look like:
pip3 
install 
torch 
torchvision

A tensor is a mathematical object used in physics, mathematics, and
computer science, particularly in the context of ML and deep
learning. Let us first understand mathematical objects. A
mathematical object can be a variable that can have a value and be
used in formulas.
Some examples of mathematical objects include points, lines,
polygons, cylinders, and cones. These can be defined using a
formula.
Now that we have understood the mathematical objects, let us dive
deep into tensors. A tensor is a multi-dimensional array or data
structure that can store and represent data in a structured way.
Tensors are a generalization of scalars (0-dimensional), vectors (1-
dimensional), and matrices (2-dimensional) to higher dimensions.
Some key characteristics and properties of tensors are as follows:
Dimensionality: Tensors can have arbitrary dimensions. The
number of dimensions in a tensor is also known as its rank or order.
For example:
A scalar is a 0-dimensional tensor.
A vector is a 1-dimensional tensor.
A matrix is a 2-dimensional tensor.
A higher-dimensional tensor can have three or more dimensions.
The diagram below shows the types of arrays and shape to illustrate
the tensor:

--- Strana 60 ---
•
•
•
•
•
Figure 2.1: Array example
Shape: The shape of a tensor describes the size of each dimension.
For example, a 2x3 matrix has a (2, 3) shape, indicating two rows
and three columns. In ML frameworks, you often encounter tensors
with shapes like (batch_size and num_features) for input data.
Data type: Tensors can store data of different types, such as
integers, floating-point numbers, or even more complex data types.
The data type determines the precision and range of values the
tensor represents.
Operations: Tensors support all mathematical operations, such as
addition, multiplication, and elementwise. In deep learning, these
operations are used for forward and backward passes during neural
network training.
Memory layout: Tensors are stored in memory in a contiguous
block, and the layout of elements is determined by the tensor's shape
and the memory layout conventions of the framework being used.
GPU acceleration: Many deep learning frameworks, including
PyTorch and TensorFlow, provide GPU support for tensor

--- Strana 61 ---
•
•
operations, allowing for faster numerical computations by
leveraging the parallel processing capabilities of modern GPUs.
In deep learning, data is often represented as tensors and neural
network models are composed of layers that take tensors as input
and produce tensors as output. Tensors store input data, model
parameters (weights and biases), intermediate activations, and
gradients during training.
Understanding tensors and their manipulation is fundamental to
working with ML and deep learning frameworks, as most operations
in these frameworks involve the manipulation of tensors to perform
computations, update model parameters, and make predictions.
Tensors are like NumPy’s ndarrays, except that tensors can run on
GPUs or other hardware accelerators. Tensors and NumPy arrays
often share the same underlying memory, eliminating the need to
copy data.
We will import numpy as np as below:
import torch
import numpy as np
Initializing a tensor
Tensors can be initialized in various ways. Look at the following examples:
Directly from data: Tensors can be created directly from data. The
data type is automatically inferred, as follows:
data = [[1, 2],[3, 4]]
x_data = torch.tensor(data)
From a NumPy array: Tensors can be created from NumPy arrays,
as follows:
np_array = np.array(data)

Operations on tensors
Over 100 tensor operations, including arithmetic, linear algebra, matrix
manipulation (transposing, indexing, slicing), sampling, and more, can be
executed.
These operations can be run on the GPU (at typically higher speeds than on
a CPU). If you are using Colab, allocate a GPU by going to Runtime |
Change runtime type | GPU.
By default, tensors are created on the CPU by default. We need to explicitly
move them to the GPU using the .to method (after checking for GPU
availability). Remember that copying large tensors across devices can be
expensive in terms of time and memory!
The following code snippet is a conditional operation that moves a tensor to
the GPU to leverage faster computing capabilities, but only if a GPU with

deep learning workflows to enhance performance during model training and
inference:

tensor = tensor.to("cuda")

Joining tensor
You can use torch.cat to concatenate a sequence of tensors along a given
dimension. torch.stack is another tensor joining operator subtly different
from torch.cat.
Tensor joining operations, like torch.cat and torch.stack, merge tensors
along a specified axis. torch.cat concatenates tensors without adding a new
dimension, while torch.stack combines tensors into a new dimension,
increasing the tensor's rank.
print("original tensor shape:" , tensor.shape)
t1 = torch.cat([tensor, tensor, tensor], dim=1)
print("joined tensor shape:" , t1.shape)
print("joined tensor:",t1)
Output:

Arithmetic operations
In PyTorch, arithmetic operations on tensors include adding, subtracting,
multiplying, and dividing. These can be performed element-wise between

--- Strana 65 ---
tensors or between tensors and scalars, automatically applying the operation
to each element, making it easy to perform mathematical computations on
multi-dimensional data.
The following lines of code show different matrix multiplications:
# This computes the matrix multiplication between two tensors. y1, y2, y3
will have the same value
# ``tensor.T`` returns the transpose of a tensor
y1 = tensor @ tensor.T
y2 = tensor.matmul(tensor.T)
y3 = torch.rand_like(y1)
torch.matmul(tensor, tensor.T, out=y3)
torch.allclose(y1,y2) and torch.allclose(y2,y3) and torch.allclose(y1,y3)
Input:
True
# This computes the element-wise product. z1, z2, z3 will have the same
value
z1 = tensor * tensor
z2 = tensor.mul(tensor)
z3 = torch.rand_like(tensor)
torch.mul(tensor, tensor, out=z3)

In-place operations
Operations that store the result in the operand are called in-place. They are
denoted by a _ suffix. For example: x.copy_(y), x.t_(), will change x.
The following lines of code first show the contents of a tensor, then increase
each element in the tensor by 5 and show the updated tensor:

Bridge with NumPy
PyTorch allows seamless conversion between its tensors and NumPy arrays,
enabling interoperability for data manipulation and scientific computing
without the need for costly data copying.
Tensors on the CPU and NumPy arrays can share their underlying memory
locations; changing one will change the other.
Tensor to NumPy array
Converting a PyTorch tensor to a NumPy array lets you switch from
PyTorch's data structure to NumPy's, making it easier to use a wide range of
scientific computing tools.
The following lines of code first create the tensor, show its contents, then
convert it into numpy and show the numpy array.
t = torch.ones(5)
print(f"t: {t}")
n = t.numpy()
print(f”n: {n}”)
Output:
t: tensor([1., 1., 1., 1., 1.])
n: [1. 1. 1. 1. 1.]
A change in the tensor is reflected in the NumPy array, as shown in the
following code:
t.add_(1)
print(f"t: {t}")

print(f”n: {n}”)
Output:
t: tensor([2., 2., 2., 2., 2.])
n: [2. 2. 2. 2. 2.]
NumPy array to tensor
Turning a NumPy array into a PyTorch tensor changes its format to
integrate it with PyTorch's deep learning tools and operations into neural
network workflows.
The following lines of code first create the NumPy array, then convert it
into tensor and show the NumPy array and tensor.
n = np.ones(5)
t = torch.from_numpy(n)
Changes in the NumPy array are reflected in the tensor.
np.add(n, 1, out=n)
print(f"t: {t}")
print(f"n: {n}")
Output:
t: tensor([2., 2., 2., 2., 2.], dtype=torch.float64)
n: [2. 2. 2. 2. 2.]

Loading a dataset
In PyTorch, loading a dataset is about setting up your data in a structured
way so it is ready for use in training or testing ML models. This process
begins by identifying where your data is, which could be files on your
computer or a remote database. You then organize this data into a Dataset
object, a PyTorch construct that standardizes how data is formatted and
accessed. Lastly, you often use a DataLoader, which takes your Dataset
and facilitates easy iteration over the data in manageable chunks or batches.

--- Strana 70 ---
This is crucial for efficient training, as it allows for seamless data
manipulation and iteration without overwhelming your system's memory.
Here is an example of how to load the FashionMNIST dataset from
torchvision. FashionMNIST is a dataset of Zalando’s article images
comprising 60,000 training and 10,000 test examples.
import torch
from torch.utils.data import Dataset
from torchvision import datasets
from torchvision.transforms import ToTensor
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
training_data = datasets.FashionMNIST(
root="data",
train=True,
download=True,
transform=ToTensor()
)
test_data = datasets.FashionMNIST(
root="data",
train=False,
download=True,
transform=ToTensor()
)
This code snippet is used for loading the FashionMNIST dataset, which is
a collection of fashion-related images used for training and testing ML
models in image recognition tasks:

can see what they look like. This process is helpful for understanding your
data better and ensuring it is in the right format before you start training a
model.

Building the neural network model
The torch.nn namespace provides all the building blocks you need to build
your neural network. Every module in PyTorch subclasses the nn.Module.
A neural network is a module that consists of other modules (layers). This
nested structure allows for building and managing complex architectures
easily.
In the following sections, we will build a neural network to classify images
in the FashionMNIST dataset.
Refer to the following code:
import os

•
•
import torch
from torch import nn
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
from torchvision import datasets, transforms
Getting a device for training
We want to train our model on a hardware accelerator like the GPU or
MPS, 
if 
available. 
Let 
us 
check 
to 
see 
if 
torch.cuda 
or
torch.backends.mps are available; otherwise, we use the CPU.
device = (
"cuda"
if torch.cuda.is_available()
else "mps"
if torch.backends.mps.is_available()
else "cpu"
)
print(f"Using {device} device")
This code snippet is about setting up the computing device for PyTorch
operations based on your system's hardware availability. It uses conditional
expressions to select the device.
The device can be a GPU (using CUDA or MPS) or a CPU:
If CUDA is available, device is set to cuda.
If CUDA is not available, it then checks for MPS availability. Metal
Performance Shaders (MPS), Apple framework for using GPU
acceleration on Mac devices. If MPS is available, the device is set to
mps.

--- Strana 76 ---
•
If neither CUDA nor MPS is available, it defaults to using the CPU
for computations by setting the device to cpu.
In summary, this code automatically selects the best available computing
device for PyTorch in the following priority: CUDA GPU, MPS GPU, and
then CPU, and prints out the selected device.
Define the class
We define our neural network by subclassing nn.Module, and initialize the
neural network layers in __init__. Every nn.Module subclass implements
the operations on input data in the forward method.
Refer to the following code:
class NeuralNetwork(nn.Module):
def __init__(self):
super().__init__()
self.flatten = nn.Flatten()
self.linear_relu_stack = nn.Sequential(
nn.Linear(28*28, 512),
nn.ReLU(),
nn.Linear(512, 512),
nn.ReLU(),
nn.Linear(512, 10),
)
def forward(self, x):
x = self.flatten(x)
logits = self.linear_relu_stack(x)
return logits

--- Strana 77 ---
We create an instance of NeuralNetwork, and move it to the device, and
print its structure, as following:
model = NeuralNetwork().to(device)
print(model)
Out:
NeuralNetwork(
  (flatten): Flatten(start_dim=1, end_dim=-1)
  (linear_relu_stack): Sequential(
(0): Linear(in_features=784, out_features=512, bias=True)
(1): ReLU()
(2): Linear(in_features=512, out_features=512, bias=True)
(3): ReLU()
(4): Linear(in_features=512, out_features=10, bias=True)
  )
)
To use the model, we pass it the input data. This executes the
model.forward, along with some background operations. Do not call
model.forward() directly!
Calling the model on the input returns a 2-dimensional tensor with dim=0
corresponding to each output of 10 raw predicted values for each class and
dim=1 corresponding to the individual values of each output. We get the
prediction probabilities by passing them through an instance of the
nn.Softmax module.
X = torch.rand(1, 28, 28, device=device)
logits = model(X)
pred_probab = nn.Softmax(dim=1)(logits)

--- Strana 78 ---
•
•
•
•
•
y_pred = pred_probab.argmax(1)
print(f"Predicted class: {y_pred}")
Output:
Predicted class: tensor([4], device='mps:0')
The above code snippet demonstrates making a prediction with a trained
model in PyTorch, using a randomly generated input tensor:
X = torch.rand(1, 28, 28, device=device): This creates a random
tensor X with the shape (1, 28, 28) that represents an image with
28x28 pixels. The 1 in the shape indicates that there is only one
image in the batch. This tensor is placed on the device chosen earlier
(which could be a CPU, CUDA GPU, or MPS GPU).
logits = model(X): The random image tensor X is passed through a
pre-trained model called model. The model processes the input and
outputs logits, which are the raw, unnormalized scores for each class
that the model is predicting.
pred_probab = nn.Softmax(dim=1)(logits): The logits are passed
through a Softmax function to convert them into probabilities. The
Softmax function is applied along dim=1, turning the logits for
each class into values between 0 and 1 that sum up to 1,
representing the model's confidence in each class.
y_pred = pred_probab.argmax(1): This finds the class with the
highest probability by taking the index of the maximum value along
dimension 1 of the pred_probab tensor. This index represents the
model's predicted class for the input image.
print(f"Predicted class: {y_pred}"): Finally, the predicted class
index is printed out, indicating which class the model thinks the
random input most likely belongs to.
This code is a basic example of generating a prediction from a PyTorch
model using a single, randomly generated input image.

--- Strana 79 ---
Model layers
Let us break down the layers in the FashionMNIST model. To illustrate it,
we will take a sample minibatch of 3 images of size 28x28 and see what
happens to them as we pass them through the network. Refer to the
following code:
input_image = torch.rand(3,28,28)
print(input_image.size())
nn.Flatten
We initialize the nn.Flatten layer to convert each 2-dimensional 28x28
image into a contiguous array of 784 pixel values (the minibatch dimension
(at dim=0) is maintained).
Refer to the following code:
flatten = nn.Flatten()
flat_image = flatten(input_image)
print(flat_image.size())
nn.Linear
The linear layer is a module that applies a linear transformation on the input
using its stored weights and biases, as shown in the following code:
layer1 = nn.Linear(in_features=28*28, out_features=20)
hidden1 = layer1(flat_image)
print(hidden1.size())
nn.ReLU
Non-linear activations create complex mappings between the model’s
inputs and outputs. They are applied after linear transformations to
introduce non-linearity, helping neural networks learn various phenomena.

--- Strana 80 ---
In this model, we use nn.ReLU between our linear layers, but there are
other activations to introduce non-linearity in your model.
Refer to the following code:
print(f"Before ReLU: {hidden1}\n\n")
hidden1 = nn.ReLU()(hidden1)
print(f"After ReLU: {hidden1}")
nn.Sequential
nn.Sequential is an ordered container of modules. The data is passed
through all the modules in the same order as defined. You can use
sequential containers to put together a quick network like seq_modules, as
follows:
seq_modules = nn.Sequential(
flatten,
layer1,
nn.ReLU(),
nn.Linear(20, 10)
)
input_image = torch.rand(3,28,28)
logits = seq_modules(input_image)
nn.Softmax
The last linear layer of the neural network returns logits—raw values in [-
infty, infty]—which are passed to the nn.Softmax module. The logits are
scaled to values [0, 1] representing the model’s predicted probabilities for
each class. dim parameter indicates the dimension along which the values
must sum to 1.

--- Strana 81 ---
Refer to the following code:
softmax = nn.Softmax(dim=1)
pred_probab = softmax(logits)
Model parameters
Many layers inside a neural network are parameterized, that is., have
associated weights and biases optimized during training. Subclassing
nn.Module automatically tracks all fields defined inside your model object
and makes all parameters accessible using your model’s parameters() or
named_parameters() methods.
In this example, we iterate over each parameter, and print its size and a
preview of its values. Refer to the following code:
print(f"Model structure: {model}\n\n")
for name, param in model.named_parameters():
   print(f"Layer: {name} | Size: {param.size()} | Values : {param[:2]} \n")
Automatic differentiation
As part of training neural networks, parameters (model weights) are
adjusted according to the gradient of the loss function with respect to the
given parameter until the loss is minimized. The most frequently used
algorithm to do this is backpropagation.
Automatic differentiation in PyTorch is a fundamental feature that allows
you to compute gradients of tensors with respect to some scalar value.
To compute those gradients, PyTorch has a built-in differentiation engine
called torch.autograd. It supports the automatic computation of gradients
for any computational graph.
Following is a simple example in PyTorch that demonstrates automatic
differentiation:
import torch

--- Strana 82 ---
•
# Create a tensor with requires_grad=True to indicate that we want to
compute gradients for it.
x = torch.tensor([2.0], requires_grad=True)
# Perform some operations on the tensor
y = x ** 2 + 3 * x + 1
# Compute the gradient of y with respect to x
y.backward()
# The gradient of y with respect to x is stored in x.grad
print(x.grad) # Should print tensor([7.])
In this example, we create a tensor x with requires_grad=True, which tells
PyTorch to track operations on x. Then, we perform some operations to
calculate y, and finally, we call backward() on y to compute the gradient of
y with respect to x.
The result x.grad will contain the gradient of y with respect to x, which is
7. This means that if you performed gradient descent to minimize y, you
would update the value of x by subtracting seven times the learning rate.
Automatic differentiation is crucial in deep learning because it allows you
to efficiently compute gradients for complex models and perform gradient-
based optimization to train neural networks.
Optimizing model parameters
Training a model is an iterative process; in each iteration, the model makes
a guess about the output, calculates the error in its guess (loss), and collects
the derivatives of the error with respect to its parameters (as we saw in the
previous section), and optimizes these parameters using gradient descent.
First, let us define the model so you can optimize the model parameters.
Define your model: First, you must define your neural network
model. This includes defining your model’s architecture, specifying

--- Strana 83 ---
•
the layers, and initializing the model’s parameters.
The following lines of code set up a basic two-layer neural network
with a ReLU activation function between the layers, ready to be
trained on data for tasks like regression or classification, depending
on the specified input_size, hidden_size, and output_size.
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
class MyModel(nn.Module):
def __init__(self):
super(MyModel, self).__init__()
self.fc1 = nn.Linear(in_features=input_size, out_features=hi
dden_size)
self.relu = nn.ReLU()
self.fc2 = nn.Linear(in_features=hidden_size, out_features=o
utput_size)
def forward(self, x):
x = self.fc1(x)
x = self.relu(x)
x = self.fc2(x)
return x
model = MyModel()
Define loss function: Choose an appropriate loss function that
measures the error between the model's predictions and the ground
truth. Refer to the following code:
criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()

--- Strana 84 ---
•
•
○
○
○
○
Choose an optimization algorithm: Select an optimization
algorithm from PyTorch's torch.optim module. Common choices
include SGD, Adam, RMSprop, etc. Configure the optimizer with
the model.parameters and learning_rate, as follows:
optimizer = torch.optim.SGD(model.parameters(), lr=learning_rate)
Training loop: Iterate over your dataset multiple times (epochs) and
perform the following steps in each iteration:
Forward pass: Pass a batch of data through your model to
compute predictions.
Calculate loss: Use the loss function to compute the loss
between the predictions and the ground truth labels.
Backward pass: Use the backward() method to compute
gradients of the loss with respect to the model's parameters.
Update parameters: Use the optimizer to update the model's
parameters based on the computed gradients, as follows:
for epoch in range(num_epochs):
for inputs, labels in dataloader:
optimizer.zero_grad() # Clear gradients from the
previous iteration
outputs = model(inputs)
loss = criterion(outputs, labels)
loss.backward() # Compute gradients
optimizer.step()
When using nn.CrossEntropyLoss in PyTorch, it expects the input
to be raw logits rather than probabilities. This is because
nn.CrossEntropyLoss internally applies the Softmax function to
the raw logits and then calculates the negative log likelihood loss.

--- Strana 85 ---
•
•
•
•
Validation and testing: After training, evaluate your model on
validation and test sets to assess its performance. This is important
for measuring how well your model generalizes to unseen data.
During validation and testing, you should explicitly apply the
Softmax function to the raw logits to convert them into
probabilities. 
This 
is 
because, 
during 
training, 
the
nn.CrossEntropyLoss function handles the Softmax implicitly, but
for evaluation purposes (e.g., validation and testing), you typically
need to interpret the model's outputs as probabilities for each class.
Saving and loading models: You can save your trained model's
parameters to disk using torch.save() and load them back when
needed using torch.load(), as follows:
a.torch.save(model.state_dict(), 'model.pth')
model.load_state_dict(torch.load('model.pth'))
Fine-tuning: If needed, you can fine-tune your model by repeating
the training process with a smaller learning rate or a different subset
of data.
Regularization and hyperparameter tuning: Experiment with
regularization techniques (e.g., dropout, weight decay) and
hyperparameter tuning (e.g., learning rate, batch size) to improve
model performance.
Save and load the model
In this section, we will look at how to persist the model state with saving,
loading, and running model predictions.
Below is the code snippet importing torch and models:
import torch
import torchvision.models as models
Saving and loading model weights

--- Strana 86 ---
PyTorch models store the learned parameters in an internal state dictionary
called state_dict. These can be persisted via the torch.save method, as
follows:
model = models.vgg16(weights='IMAGENET1K_V1')
torch.save(model.state_dict(), 'model_weights.pth')
To load model weights, you need to create an instance of the same model
first and then load the parameters using load_state_dict() method.
Refer to the following code:
model = models.vgg16() # we do not specify ``weights``, i.e. create
untrained model
model.load_state_dict(torch.load('model_weights.pth'))
model.eval()
Saving and loading models with shapes
When loading model weights, we needed to instantiate the model class first,
because the class defines the structure of a network. We might want to save
the structure of this class together with the model, in which case we can
pass model (and not model.state_dict()) to the saving function:
torch.save(model, ‘model.pth’)
We can then load the model like this:
model = torch.load('model.pth')

Chapter 3
Multi-object Detection
Introduction
This chapter introduces the user to dealing with image data, segmenting
images, transforming images, and building deep learning models to detect
multiple objects in an image dataset. Computer vision has changed greatly,
thanks to deep learning, especially in spotting objects in images. Among
different ways to spot objects, one method called classification-based
multi-object detection is really good at not just figuring out what is in a
picture but also saying exactly where it is. In this chapter, we will talk about
this method.
It uses convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to do two jobs at once:
Figuring out what an object is and where it is located in the image. By
teaching these networks to recognize different types of objects and guess
where they are, we get a powerful way to find stuff in pictures. PyTorch is a
great tool because it is easy to use and has many helpful features.
Structure
In this chapter, we will go through the following topics:
Problem statement

--- Strana 90 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Deep dive into YOLO

Downloading dataset for only a few classes
