Objectives
In this chapter, we will discuss the basics of classification-based multi-
object detection. We will first understand details about object detection, the
difference between image classification and object detection, and use cases
of object detection. We will then understand the algorithms used for object
detection, popular datasets, and solution architecture for model training and
evaluation.
We will also give you some tips on building and training these models using
PyTorch. By the end, you will know a lot about finding multiple objects in
images and be ready to use this knowledge in your projects.
Let us first understand about computer vision. Computer vision focuses on
enabling computers to extract meaningful information from images and
videos to interpret visual data. It involves the development of algorithms
and machine learning (ML) models to replicate human vision capabilities
to process, analyze, and make decisions on visual data.
Problem statement
Given an image containing multiple objects of interest, the task is to
develop a deep learning model capable of accurately detecting and
localizing these objects within the image. This involves identifying the

--- Strana 91 ---
types of objects present and precisely outlining their bounding boxes to
indicate their locations. The challenge lies in handling various object
classes, varying sizes, occlusions, and cluttered backgrounds commonly
encountered in real-world images. The objective is to devise a robust and
efficient solution for multi-object detection that can be applied across
diverse domains such as autonomous driving, surveillance, medical
imaging, and more.
Object detection
Object detection is a computer vision task that identifies and locates objects
of interest within an image or video. The goal is to recognize what objects
are present in the visual data and provide information about where they are
located in the image. Object detection is a fundamental problem in
computer vision and has numerous real-world applications.
The image below showcases a bustling city street with pedestrians,
vehicles, traffic lights, and signage:
Figure 3.1: Image with multiple objects labeled with bounding boxes with annotation
When we talk about how computers understand pictures, they use different
parts to make sense of what they see. Imagine a computer looking at a
photo of a city street. Here is a simple breakdown of what it does:

--- Strana 92 ---
•
•
•
•
Street scene: The background typically depicts a street or road,
representing the context in which objects are detected.
Bounding boxes: Bounding boxes are drawn around individual
objects within the scene. Each box is rectangular and contains an
object of interest. The object detection algorithm determines the size
and position of the bounding boxes.
Objects: Objects inside the bounding boxes can include cars,
pedestrians, dogs, signposts, traffic lights, and other elements in the
street scene.
Labels: To further illustrate the concept, labels are often added near
or within the bounding boxes to indicate each object, e.g., car,
person, or dog.
Image classification is the process of teaching a computer to recognize and
categorize different types of images. For example, let us say we want to
classify different types of flowers based on their images. We collect photos
of daisies, dandelions, roses, sunflowers, and tulips. Then, we train a ML
model to recognize flower patterns. When we give it a new flower image, it
predicts the flower type (e.g., rose or tulip). This technology has practical
applications in fields like medical imaging, self-driving cars, and quality
control.
Object detection enables the computer to identify and locate various objects
within an image or video frame. For example, let us say we want to classify
different types of flowers contained within each image. We collect photos
containing daisies, dandelions, roses, sunflowers, and tulips. Then, we train
a ML model to recognize flower patterns. When we give it a new image, it
predicts all the flower types (e.g., rose and tulip).
Image classification vs. object detection
Let us understand the key difference between image classification and
object detection. The figures below capture a teddy bear sitting and reading
a book.

--- Strana 93 ---
•
•
•
•
•
The image classification will classify the image as a teddy bear and the
object detection will classify image as a list containing both teddy bear and
book:
Figure 3.2: Image classification vs object detection
The following are the key terms of object detection:
Bounding box: A rectangular region drawn around an object to
specify the object's location.
Class label: The category or class to which an object belongs.
Object localization: The process of determining the coordinates of
a bounding box that accurately encloses an object.
Anchor boxes: Pre-defined bounding boxes of various sizes and
aspect ratios are used in object detection models to predict object
locations.
Non-maximum suppression (NMS): A postprocessing technique
that removes redundant bounding boxes by keeping only the one
with the highest confidence score when multiple boxes overlap.

--- Strana 94 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Intersection over union (IoU): A metric that measures the overlap
between two bounding boxes. It is used to evaluate the accuracy of
bounding box predictions.
Feature pyramid network (FPN): A network architecture that
enhances the feature representation of objects at multiple scales,
helping in the detection of objects of different sizes.
Region proposal network (RPN): Part of the Faster region-based
convolutional neural networks (R-CNN) architecture, RPN
generates proposals for potential object locations in an image.
The following are the use cases and applications of object detection:
Autonomous driving: Object detection is critical for autonomous
vehicles to identify and locate objects such as other vehicles,
pedestrians, traffic signs, traffic lights, lanes, and obstacles on the
road.
Surveillance and security: Object detection is widely used for real-
time detection of intruders etc.
Medical imaging: Object detection helps locate and identify
anomalies and tumors in x-rays, MRIs, and CT scans.
Document analysis: Object detection helps locate and categorize
elements within documents, such as headings, paragraphs, tables,
and graphics.
Face detection and recognition: Object detection is used for face
detection and recognition in applications like access control and
security.
Quality control in manufacturing: Object detection can be applied
to detect defects in products on assembly lines to ensure quality.
Entertainment and gaming: Object detection is used in video
games for player tracking and gesture recognition.

--- Strana 95 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Augmented reality (AR) and virtual reality (VR): AR and VR
applications employ object detection for tracking and interacting
with the real-world and virtual objects.
Robotics: Object detection helps robots navigate and manipulate
objects in the industrial environment, making them useful in
manufacturing, logistics, and healthcare.
Algorithms used for object detection
Object detection has seen significant advancements in recent years in the
development of various algorithms and architectures to enhance the object
detection problem. Here are some of the most popular algorithms and
models used in object detection:
Faster R-CNN: Faster R-CNN is a widely used object detection
framework that combines an RPN with a CNN for object
classification. It introduced the concept of anchor boxes for more
precise bounding box predictions.
You Only Look Once (YOLO): YOLO is known for its speed and
real-time object detection capabilities. It divides the image into a
grid and assigns bounding boxes and class predictions for each grid
cell, making it faster but potentially less accurate for small objects.
Single Shot MultiBox Detector (SSD): SSD is designed for real-
time object detection by predicting multiple bounding boxes and
class probabilities at different scales within the network. It offers a
good balance between speed and accuracy.
RetinaNet: RetinaNet addresses the issue of imbalanced data in
object detection by introducing focal loss, which focuses on hard
and misclassified examples. It combines a FPN with a classification
and regression subnetwork.
Mask R-CNN: An extension of Faster R-CNN, Mask R-CNN adds
a branch to predict pixel-wise segmentation masks for each object in

--- Strana 96 ---
•
•
•
•
•
addition to bounding boxes and class labels. It is widely used, for
instance, segmentation.
EfficientDet: EfficientDet is known for its efficiency and high
performance, achieved by optimizing the architecture and scaling
the model. It introduces compound scaling to balance model depth,
width, and resolution.
Region-based fully convolutional network (R-FCN): R-FCN
introduces position-sensitive score maps to improve the localization
accuracy of objects. It combines the benefits of RPN and fully
convolutional networks (FCN).
YOLOv4, YOLOv5, YOLOv6, YOLOv7: The YOLO algorithm is
a state-of-the-art, real-time object detection system that views
images in a single pass, quickly and accurately identifying and
classifying various objects through bounding boxes and labels. Until
YOLOv5, it divides the image into a grid system, where each grid
cell predicts a certain number of bounding boxes, including their
confidence scores and class probabilities, allowing the algorithm to
localize and detect multiple objects in an image simultaneously.
Data-efficient Transformer (DETR): DETR replaces CNNs with
transformers for end-to-end object detection. It demonstrates the
potential of transformers in vision tasks.
CornerNet and CenterNet: CornerNet is a different way to find
objects in pictures. Unlike usual methods, it looks for the corners of
objects—where the top-left and bottom-right points meet. It uses
heat maps that spot these corners as hot spots on the image, making
it easier to say where the object is. CenterNet simplifies finding
objects in pictures. It looks for the middle spot of an object and
calculates its size and placement from there. This method does not
use the common steps of drawing boxes around objects, making the
process less complicated.

--- Strana 97 ---
•
•
High-resolution network (HRNet): HRNet is a way to process
images that keeps the details sharp and clear throughout the process.
Instead of making the image smaller and then trying to make it
detailed again, HRNet maintains the high-resolution, making sure
that even the tiniest details are captured accurately.
HRNet works by keeping multiple high-to-low resolution pathways
and constantly exchanging information between them. This means it
can recognize both the big picture and the fine details
simultaneously, which is great for spotting specific things in an
image, like facial features or individual fingers.
Lidar-based object detection: For autonomous driving and
robotics, lidar data can be fused with images using algorithms like
PointPillars, PV-RCNN, or PointRCNN for 3D object detection.
Lidar-based object detection uses laser beams to measure distances
and create a detailed 3D map of the surrounding area. This
technology can spot objects around it by how the lasers bounce
back, helping it to understand exactly where things are and how far
away they are.
This method is like giving a car or robot a special set of eyes that can see in
3D, even in the dark or bad weather. Lidar's performance may be affected
by rainy weather. It helps self-driving cars avoid obstacles and robots move
around safely by accurately detecting and identifying objects all around
them.
Algorithm selection
The choice of algorithm depends on the specific requirements of the
application, including factors such as speed, accuracy, and the nature of the
objects to be detected.
The choice of which object detection algorithm to use depends on several
factors, including the specific requirements of your application, the
available computational resources, and the trade-offs between speed and

--- Strana 98 ---
•
○
○
•
○
○
•
○
○
•
○
•
accuracy. Here are some guidelines to help you decide which algorithm to
use for object detection:
Speed vs. accuracy:
If your application requires real-time object detection and
speed is a top priority, you might consider models like
YOLOv10. These models are optimized for speed and can
provide good results.
If accuracy is the primary concern and real-time performance is
not a strict requirement, you may opt for slower but more
accurate models like Dynamic R-CNN, Faster R-CNN or
RetinaNet, or transformer-based models.
Model efficiency:
Consider 
the 
computational 
resources 
available 
for
deployment. Smaller models, such as MobileNet-based
architectures, may be more suitable for edge devices with
limited processing power.
EfficientDet is designed to balance accuracy and efficiency by
scaling model depth, width, and resolution.
Small object detection:
If your task involves detecting small objects, you may need
models that are specifically designed to handle such scenarios.
EfficientDet and HRNet are known for their effectiveness in
small object detection.
HRNet focuses on detecting small objects in high-resolution
images.
Instance segmentation:
If your task involves not only object detection but also pixel-
wise segmentation of object instances, models like Mask R-
CNN, U-net architecture are well-suited for this purpose.
Real-time video processing:

--- Strana 99 ---
○
•
○
○
•
○
○
•
○
○
•
○
For real-time video analysis or tracking, consider models that
combine object detection with object tracking, such as simple
online and real-time tracking (SORT), fair multi-object
tracking (FairMOT).
Robustness to data imbalance:
In situations where your dataset is highly imbalanced (e.g.,
many negative examples and few positives), models like
RetinaNet with focal loss may perform better.
RetinaNet is a remarkable one-stage object detection model
that leverages the focal loss function to address class imbalance
during training.
Accuracy with low false positives:
If minimizing false positives (i.e., incorrect object detections)
is a critical requirement, consider models that are designed to
reduce false positives, such as Cascade R-CNN.
Cascade R-CNN extends the popular two-stage R-CNN object
detection framework. It aims to obtain high-quality detections
by effectively rejecting close false positives.
Specific domain requirements:
Certain domains have specialized object detection models
designed to meet their specific needs. For example, PointPillars
and PV-RCNN are used for 3D object detection in autonomous
driving applications.
PointPillars uses vertical columns (pillars) and is efficient 3D
object detection from point clouds. PV-RCNN integrates voxel
CNN and PointNet-based set abstraction and achieves
impressive results using point clouds alone.
Experimental evaluation:
It is often a good practice to experiment with multiple models
and evaluate their performance on your specific dataset to
determine which one works best for your application.

--- Strana 100 ---
•
○
•
○
•
Model size and footprint:
Consider the available storage capacity and model size
limitations, especially in edge computing scenarios where
model size is a concern.
Transfer learning:
Transfer learning can be a powerful approach. If you have
access to a pre-trained model on a large dataset, fine-tuning it
for your specific task can save time and improve performance.
In practice, there is no one-size-fits-all answer, and the choice of an object
detection algorithm depends on the unique characteristics, constraints, and
performance requirements. It is often beneficial to experiment with different
models and assess their performance on a specific dataset and use cases to
make an informed decision. Additionally, model ensembles or combining
multiple models can be an effective strategy to achieve a balance between
speed and accuracy.
Object detection types
The following are the object detection types:
Two-stage detectors: Two-stage detectors first generate region
proposals and then classify these regions into object categories.
These methods are typically more accurate but slower compared to
single-stage detectors.
Example: Faster R-CNN is a classic two-stage detector. It first uses
a RPN to suggest potential object locations. Then, in the second
stage, these proposed regions are refined and classified by a CNN-
based detector.
Advantages: High accuracy due to the two-step refinement process.
Disadvantages: Slower inference speed, making it less suitable for
real-time applications.

--- Strana 101 ---
•
•
•
Single-stage detectors: Single-stage detectors predict bounding
boxes and class labels directly in a single forward pass without
needing a separate region proposal step. These models prioritize
speed and are often used in real-time applications.
Example: YOLO and SSD are both single-stage detectors. YOLO
divides the image into a grid and directly predicts bounding boxes
and class probabilities, while SSD performs similar operations
across multiple feature maps at different scales.
Advantages: Faster inference speed, making them ideal for real-
time applications like self-driving cars and video surveillance.
Disadvantages: Generally lower accuracy compared to two-stage
detectors, especially in handling small objects or complex scenes.
Anchor-based detectors: Anchor-based detectors use pre-defined
bounding boxes (anchor boxes) to predict object locations. These
anchor boxes are placed at various locations in the image, and the
network predicts offsets relative to these anchors to fine-tune the
bounding box placement.
Example: Faster R-CNN and SSD are both anchor-based detectors.
They use multiple anchor boxes with different aspect ratios and
sizes, and the network refines these anchors to match the object’s
actual bounding box.
Advantages: Flexibility in detecting objects of different sizes and
aspect ratios.
Disadvantages: Requires more computation to predict offsets for a
large number of anchors, increasing complexity.
Anchorless detectors: Anchorless detectors eliminate the need for
pre-defined anchor boxes and instead predict key points, object
centers, or corners directly to infer bounding boxes. This makes the
process simpler and often more efficient.

--- Strana 102 ---
•
•
•
○
Example: CornerNet and CenterNet are anchorless detectors. In
CornerNet, the network predicts the top-left and bottom-right
corners of the objects, while CenterNet predicts the center of the
object and uses it to infer the bounding box.
Advantages: Reduced complexity due to the absence of anchor
boxes, potentially faster and more efficient.
Disadvantages: Can struggle with localization accuracy, especially
for small objects or crowded scenes.
Single-stage object detection neural network
The base architecture for a single-stage object detection neural network
typically includes three main components:
Backbone (feature extractor): The backbone network is
responsible for extracting hierarchical features from the input image.
Popular backbones include CNNs such as ResNet, VGG,
MobileNet, or EfficientNet. These networks are pretrained on large
datasets like ImageNet to learn useful features. The output of the
backbone is a feature map, which provides different levels of
information, such as edges, textures, and objects.
Neck (FPN or SPP): Many single-stage detectors use a neck
structure like a FPN or spatial pyramid pooling (SPP) to enhance
feature maps. The purpose of the neck is to capture multi-scale
features and provide fine-to-coarse information across the layers. It
helps detect objects of varying sizes effectively by combining low-
level detailed features and high-level abstracted features.
Head (classification and regression layers): The detection head is
a series of convolutional layers that generate predictions, which
consist of:
Bounding box regression: Predicting the coordinates of the
bounding boxes around objects in the image.

--- Strana 103 ---
•
•
•
Object classification: Classifying the objects within the bounding
boxes.
These layers directly output the class labels and bounding box
predictions for the object without the need for an additional region
proposal stage (as in two-stage detectors like Faster R-CNN).
The following figure shows the various components of a single-
stage object detection neural network:
Figure 3.3: Single-stage object detection neural network
Feature pyramid network architecture
A critical challenge in object detection is detecting objects of varying sizes
within the same image. FPN solves this by creating a multi-scale
representation of the image using a top-down architecture with lateral
connections.
In FPN:
The bottom-up pathway extracts feature maps from different layers
of a convolutional neural network (e.g., ResNet).
The top-down pathway upsamples these feature maps and merges
them with higher-resolution feature maps from earlier layers.

--- Strana 104 ---
•
•
1.
2.
Lateral connections refine these combined features to ensure that the
model can detect objects at different scales.
By using FPNs, modern detectors like Faster R-CNN and RetinaNet
significantly improve their ability to detect small objects, which
would otherwise be missed. FPNs are particularly important in tasks
like face detection or medical imaging, where small details are
critical.
Solution architecture
An object detection pipeline is a series of steps or processes designed to
detect and locate objects within an image or video. Here is an overview of a
typical object detection pipeline:
Figure 3.4: Object detection pipeline
Input data: The pipeline begins with input data, which can be
images or video frames. This data is captured by cameras or other
sensors.
Preprocessing: 
Preprocessing 
steps 
may 
include 
resizing,
normalization, and enhancement of the input data to make it suitable
for object detection. Preprocessing can improve the algorithm's
performance by reducing noise and standardizing input data.

--- Strana 105 ---
3.
4.
5.
6.
7.
8.
9.
10.
Feature extraction: Feature extraction is the process of selecting
relevant information from the input data. Features can include
edges, corners, textures, and other visual cues that help distinguish
objects from the background.
Object detection model: The heart of the pipeline is the object
detection model. This model can be based on traditional computer
vision techniques or deep learning. Deep learning-based models,
such as Faster R-CNN, YOLO, or SSD, have gained popularity due
to their accuracy.
Training (Optional): In some cases, you may need to train the
object detection model on a labeled dataset. Training involves
optimizing the model's parameters to make accurate predictions.
Inference: Inference is the process of running the trained model on
new, unlabeled data. The model generates predictions, typically in
the form of bounding boxes around detected objects.
Postprocessing: Postprocessing steps are applied to the model's
predictions to refine and interpret the results. This may include
NMS to eliminate duplicate detections and filtering based on object
confidence scores.
Output: The final output of the object detection pipeline is a list of
objects along with their locations in the image or video. This
information can be used for various applications, such as tracking,
counting, or further analysis.
Visualization: To make the results more interpretable, you can
visualize the detected objects by drawing bounding boxes and labels
on the input data.
Decision or action (Application-specific): Depending on the
application, the pipeline's output may trigger specific actions or
decisions. For example, in autonomous vehicles, detected objects
may influence driving decisions.

--- Strana 106 ---
11.
•
•
•
Feedback (Optional): In some cases, feedback may be sent to the
system to improve object detection accuracy. This can include
retraining the model with new data.
Multi-object detection loss computation
In multi-object detection, the loss function plays a vital role in training the
model. For models like YOLO, the loss is computed using a combination
of:
Localization loss: This measures how accurately the model predicts
the location and size of the bounding box compared to the ground
truth.
Confidence loss: This penalizes incorrect predictions about whether
an object exists in a given grid cell or bounding box. It ensures that
the model correctly distinguishes between object-containing and
empty regions.
Classification loss: This measures how well the model predicts the
correct class of the detected objects.
Together, these loss components ensure that the network learns to localize
objects (bounding boxes) and classify them correctly. During training, the
model matches predictions with ground truth using the IoU metric, which
acts as the guiding factor for assigning bounding boxes to ground truth
objects.
This loss function, coupled with IoU and confidence scores, forms the core
learning mechanism in object detection models.
Deep dive into YOLO
YOLO is a popular real-time object detection algorithm known for its speed
and efficiency. It works by dividing an image into a grid and making object
predictions for each grid cell.

--- Strana 107 ---
1.
•
•
•
•
An analogy for YOLO in the context of object detection is to think of it as a
talented chef in a busy restaurant who can quickly scan a fully loaded tray
of dishes and accurately identify the type of each dish with just one glance.
Here is how the analogy works:
The busy restaurant: Imagine a busy restaurant where a constant
stream of dishes emerges from the kitchen. The environment is
hectic, and the task is to identify what is on each plate as rapidly as
possible:
The talented chef (YOLO): YOLO embodies the talented
chef. Instead of meticulously inspecting each plate individually,
YOLO takes a quick glance at the entire tray of dishes all at
once.
One-look, many dishes: With a single look, the chef identifies
the type of each dish on the tray and mentally notes what is
present. There is no need to revisit individual plates; everything
is comprehended in one go.
Efficiency and speed: This one-look approach is exceptionally
efficient and lightning-fast, enabling the chef to keep pace with
the restaurant’s demands. Similarly, YOLO processes an entire
image in a single pass, making it suitable for real-time object
detection in scenarios like autonomous vehicles and security
surveillance.
Accuracy and confidence: Just as the chef’s years of
experience instill confidence in their ability to identify dishes
accurately, YOLO provides confidence scores for its object
detection predictions. These scores indicate how certain YOLO
is about the detected objects.
Here is a deep dive into how YOLO works, including annotations,
model architecture, prediction structure, loss computation, and
NMS.

--- Strana 108 ---
2.
•
•
•
3.
•
•
4.
Annotations and predictions: During training, annotations (ground
truth) specify the bounding box coordinates and class labels for
objects in the image. YOLO divides an image into an SxS grid, with
each grid cell predicting multiple bounding boxes and their
respective object class probabilities. These predictions include:
Bounding box coordinates (x, y, width, height) relative to the
grid cell.
Objectness score, which indicates the likelihood that an object
exists in the predicted box.
Class probabilities, determining the type of object detected.
Model architecture: YOLO uses a FCN with a CNN backbone
(e.g., CSPDarknet53) for feature extraction. YOLO’s architecture
includes anchor boxes, which are pre-defined bounding boxes with
various sizes and aspect ratios, helping the model predict bounding
boxes of objects of different shapes and sizes. The model's
prediction head outputs a tensor containing bounding box
coordinates, objectness scores, and class probabilities for each grid
cell.
YOLO applies:
Sigmoid activation for the bounding box center (x, y) and
objectness score.
Exponentiation for width and height to scale predictions
relative to the anchor box.
Matching predictions with ground truth: Predictions are
compared with the ground truth bounding boxes using IoU. IoU
measures the overlap between predicted and actual bounding boxes.
If the IoU exceeds a certain threshold, the prediction is considered a
match. IoU is computed as:
IoU= Area of overlap × Area of union

--- Strana 109 ---
5.
•
•
•
6.
•
•
•
Loss function: YOLO's loss function is composed of three
components:
Localization loss: Measures the error between predicted and
ground truth bounding box coordinates.
Confidence loss: Penalizes incorrect predictions of objectness
(both false positives and negatives).
Classification loss: Evaluates the error in classifying objects in
the bounding boxes.
Together, these components ensure that YOLO learns both object
localization and classification.
NMS: After prediction, YOLO applies NMS to remove redundant
bounding boxes. NMS keeps the box with the highest confidence
score and suppresses others with significant overlap, determined by
the IoU threshold. This process ensures that each object in the image
is represented by a single, high-confidence bounding box.
Here is an overview of the working mechanism of YOLO:
Grid division: YOLO divides the input image into a grid,
typically using a 7x7 or 13x13 grid, depending on the YOLO
version used. Each grid cell represents a spatial region in the
image, and YOLO makes predictions for objects within these
cells.
Bounding box predictions: For each grid cell, YOLO predicts
bounding boxes around objects. These bounding boxes are
represented by coordinates (x, y) for the center of the box and
(width, height) for its dimensions. YOLO also predicts a
confidence score for each bounding box, which indicates how
likely the box contains an object.
Class predictions: YOLO predicts the class probabilities for
the object contained in each bounding box. This means that, for
each grid cell, the algorithm assigns probabilities to different
classes or object categories.

--- Strana 110 ---
•
○
After making predictions for all grid cells, YOLO applies NMS to filter out
redundant or overlapping bounding boxes. NMS keeps only the box with
the highest confidence score when multiple boxes overlap.
Output: The final output of YOLO consists of a list of bounding boxes,
confidence scores, and class labels for all detected objects in the image.
Anchor boxes: YOLO improves the localization of objects by introducing
the concept of anchor boxes. These anchor boxes are predefined bounding
boxes of different sizes and aspect ratios. Modern YOLO architectures are
anchorless object detectors. During training, the model learns to adjust
these anchor boxes to better fit the objects in the training dataset:
Figure 3.5: Anchor boxes
Over the years, YOLO has seen several versions and improvements, with
each version enhancing speed and accuracy. It has been widely adopted in
applications such as autonomous driving, surveillance, and robotics, where
real-time object detection is crucial.

Data preprocessing
Data preprocessing for training on the COCO dataset typically involves
several steps to prepare the images and annotations for object detection or

--- Strana 121 ---
•
•
segmentation tasks. Here is a common data preprocessing pipeline for the
COCO dataset before training:
Image resizing and normalization: Resize the images to a
consistent size that the chosen object detection model expects.
Common sizes include 416x416 or 608x608. You can use libraries
like OpenCV or Pillow to perform this task. The code below imports
the Python library and defines the function to resize the images and
normalize the pixel values to the range [0,1]:
import cv2
def preprocess_image(image_path, target_size):
image = cv2.imread(image_path)
image = cv2.resize(image, target_size)
image = image / 255.0
return image
Bounding box normalization: Normalize the bounding box
coordinates to the range [0, 1] relative to the image size. This is
necessary for consistency across images with different resolutions.
The code below defines the function to normalize the bounding box
coordinates:
def normalize_bbox(bbox, image_width, image_height):
x, y, width, height = bbox
x /= image_width
y /= image_height
width /= image_width
height /= image_height
return [x, y, width, height]

--- Strana 122 ---
•
○
○
Generate training data: For each image, extract the bounding box
annotations and their corresponding class labels. This data is
typically in the format expected by your object detection model. The
below code defines the function to generate the training data takes
two arguments:
coco: A COCO dataset object.
image_id: The unique identifier of the image for which
training data is being generated.
It loads the image and its annotations, constructs the image path,
preprocess each image to resize to 416x416 pixels, normalizes the
bounding boxes and finally extracts the class label for each
annotated object:
def generate_training_data(coco, image_id):
image_info = coco.loadImgs(image_id)[0]
annotations 
=
coco.loadAnns(coco.getAnnIds(imgIds=image_id))
image_path = os.path.join(image_dir, image_info['file_name'])
image = preprocess_image(image_path, (416, 416))
boxes 
= 
[normalize_bbox(ann['bbox'], 
image.shape[1],
image.shape[0]) for ann in annotations]
class_labels = [ann['category_id'] for ann in annotations]
return image, boxes, class_labels
Model training
Model training on the COCO dataset typically involves several steps to load
the COCO preprocessed dataset, transforming the dataset, defining the
YOLO model, setting the training parameters and optimizer, training the
model iteratively, and finally saving the model:

#The code below imports necessary libraries:
import torch
import torchvision
from torchvision.models.detection import fasterrcnn_resnet50_fpn
from torchvision.transforms import functional as F
from torchvision.datasets import CocoDetection
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
#The code below defines data transformations
transform = torchvision.transforms.Compose([
torchvision.transforms.ToTensor(),])
#The code below loads the COCO dataset (training set)
coco_train_dataset 
= 
CocoDetection(root="path/to/coco/train2017",
annFile="path/to/coco/annotations/instances_train2017.json",
transform=transform)
#The code below creates a DataLoader for training
train_dataloader 
= 
DataLoader(coco_train_dataset, 
batch_size=2,
shuffle=True, num_workers=4)
#The code below defines the YOLO model
model = fasterrcnn_resnet50_fpn(pretrained=True)
#The code below sets training parameters and optimizer
params = [p for p in model.parameters() if p.requires_grad]
optimizer 
= 
torch.optim.SGD(params, 
lr=0.005, 
momentum=0.9,
weight_decay=0.0005)

--- Strana 124 ---
lr_scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.StepLR(optimizer, step_size=3,
gamma=0.1)
#The code below trains the model
model.train()
for epoch in range(10):
for images, targets in train_dataloader:
images = list(image for image in images)
targets = [{k: v for k, v in t.items()} for t in targets]
loss_dict = model(images, targets)
losses = sum(loss for loss in loss_dict.values())
optimizer.zero_grad()
losses.backward()
optimizer.step()
lr_scheduler.step()
#The code below saves the trained model
torch.save(model.state_dict(), "yolo_coco_model.pth")

Model testing and evaluation
IoU is one of the key metrics in object detection, used to measure how well
the predicted bounding boxes overlap with the ground truth boxes.
Understanding IoU is crucial because it is used during both the training
phase (to assign bounding boxes to ground truth objects) and the evaluation
phase (to measure the accuracy of predictions).
When a model predicts a bounding box for an object, IoU compares the
area of overlap between the predicted and actual boxes with the area of their
union. Mathematically, IoU is represented as:
For example, if the IoU threshold is set to 0.5, a predicted box is considered
correct if the IoU with the ground truth box is at least 50%. This connects
directly to mAP, where IoU thresholds (e.g., IoU@0.5, IoU@0.75) are used
to evaluate a model’s performance.
Non-maximum suppression algorithm
One of the challenges in object detection is handling multiple predictions
for the same object. This is where NMS plays a critical role. After the
network generates multiple bounding boxes for each object, some of these
boxes may overlap significantly, even though they represent the same
object. NMS ensures that only the best bounding box (the one with the
highest confidence score) is kept, and others are suppressed.
The process of NMS involves:
Sorting all predicted bounding boxes by their confidence scores.
Removing boxes that have a high overlap (measured using IoU)
with a more confident box.

--- Strana 127 ---
3.
•
•
•
Repeating this process for all objects in the image.
When evaluating an object detection model trained using YOLO on the
COCO dataset, you typically use the following evaluation metrics:
mAP: mAP is a widely used metric for object detection and
measures the average precision across different object categories. It
quantifies how well the model identifies and localizes objects in
images. The COCO evaluation metric uses IoU thresholds at 0.5,
0.55, 0.6, 0.65, 0.7, 0.75, 0.8, 0.85, and 0.9.
mAP@IoU0.50: This is the mAP calculated using a single IoU
threshold of 0.50. It measures the model's performance when
considering detections that have an IoU of 0.50 or higher with the
ground truth.
mAP@IoU0.75: This is the mAP calculated using a single IoU
threshold of 0.75. It measures the model's performance when
considering detections with a higher degree of overlap (IoU ≥ 0.75)
with the ground truth.
In an object detection problem, a false positive occurs when the model
makes an incorrect detection. This can happen in two ways: The model
either detects an object in an area where no object is present, or it correctly
identifies the location of an object but assigns the wrong class label. Both
situations lead to a false positive, lowering the model's precision.
A false negative occurs when the model fails to correctly detect an object
that is present in the image. This can happen in two ways: The model either
completely misses the object by not predicting any bounding box, or it
predicts a bounding box that has insufficient overlap with the ground truth,

--- Strana 128 ---
•
•
•
•
•
falling below the required IoU threshold. Both scenarios result in a false
negative, reducing the model's recall.
Average recall: The average recall measures the average recall
across different object categories. It provides insights into how well
the model detects objects in the dataset.
Precision-recall (PR) curve: The PR curve visualizes the trade-off
between precision and recall at various confidence thresholds. It is
useful for understanding the model's performance at different
detection confidence levels.
F1 score: The F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision and
recall. It provides a balanced measure of the model's performance in
object detection.
False positive rate (FPR): FPR measures the ratio of false positive
predictions to the actual negatives, indicating the model's propensity
to produce incorrect detections.
Miss rate (MR): MR measures the ratio of missed detections to the
actual positives, indicating the model's ability to find all objects in
the image.

--- Strana 129 ---
Evaluating the performance of a YOLO model using the COCO dataset
typically involves using the official COCO evaluation tools. Here is an
example of how you can evaluate the performance:
import subprocess
#The code below defines the paths to the COCO evaluation toolkit and

'python', os.path.join(coco_eval_dir, 'eval.py'),
coco_annotation_file,
'-g', 'yolo', # Use YOLO format detections
'-i', yolo_model,
'-o', 'results.json', # Output the results to a JSON file
'--img-size', '416', # Specify the image size used during training
'--conf-thres', '0.001', # Set confidence threshold

]

#The code below calculates and print mAP using the generated results

with open(result_file, 'r') as file:
lines = file.readlines()
#The code below searches for the mAP line in the results file
for line in lines:
if 'mAP' in line:
mAP_line = linermance of a YOLO model trained on the COCO
dataset:
#The code below extracts mAP value from the line
mAP_value = float(mAP_line.split('=')[-1].strip())
print(f'mAP: {mAP_value}')

Improving object detection using YOLO on the COCO dataset can be
challenging but rewarding. Here are several strategies and techniques to
enhance the performance of the object detection model:
Larger and more diverse training data: Consider adding more
diverse and extensive training data to improve the model's ability to
detect objects in various scenarios and conditions. You can collect
additional data or use external datasets for object classes relevant to
your application.
Data augmentation: Apply data augmentation techniques to the
training dataset, including random cropping, rotation, flipping, and
color adjustments. Data augmentation increases the diversity of
training examples, helping the model generalize better.
Fine-tuning: Fine-tune a pre-trained YOLO model using transfer
learning. You can start with a model trained on a larger dataset and
then fine-tune it on the COCO dataset for your specific object
classes.
Hyperparameter 
tuning: 
Experiment 
with 
different
hyperparameters such as learning rate, batch size, optimizer, anchor
sizes, and confidence thresholds. Hyperparameter tuning can
significantly impact model performance.
Larger model architectures: Use the most recent YOLO and
complex model architectures that may offer improved performance
over the older YOLO. Consider trying these architectures.
Ensemble methods: Combine predictions from multiple YOLO
models to improve detection accuracy. Ensemble methods can
provide more robust results.

--- Strana 132 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Postprocessing techniques: Implement postprocessing techniques
to refine detections, such as NMS, and filter out low-confidence
predictions.
Analyze and address common errors: Carefully analyze the types
of errors your model is making (e.g., false positives, false negatives)
and tailor your training strategy to address these specific issues.
Regularization techniques: Implement regularization techniques
like dropout or weight decay to prevent overfitting and improve
model generalization.
Use custom anchors: Anchors are predefined bounding boxes of
different sizes and aspect ratios used to detect objects of varying
scales in images. If the default anchor sizes are not suitable for your
dataset, consider creating custom anchor sizes based on your object
classes.
Object tracking: Incorporate object tracking methods to maintain
object identities across frames in video applications.
Semantic/instance 
segmentation: 
Consider 
using 
semantic
segmentation alongside object detection to improve the accuracy of
object boundaries and localization.
Class imbalance handling: Address class imbalance issues by
oversampling or undersampling object classes based on their
prevalence in the dataset.
Custom loss functions: Design and implement custom loss
functions that focus on specific aspects of detection accuracy
relevant to your application.
Quantization and compression: Apply quantization and model
compression techniques to deploy your model on resource-
constrained platforms while maintaining performance.

--- Strana 133 ---
Remember that improving object detection using YOLO on the COCO
dataset is often an iterative process. Experiment with different approaches,
keep track of your model's performance, and continue to fine-tune until you
achieve the desired level of accuracy and precision.

In conclusion, this chapter has delved deeply into classification-based
multi-object detection, illuminating the intricacies and applications of this

exploration of CNNs and their dual role in identifying and localizing

understood the subject matter.
Furthermore, the chapter highlighted the YOLO algorithm's efficiency and

bustling restaurant to explain its one-look, multi-object detection prowess.
The journey through the COCO, Pascal VOC, and ImageNet datasets

refining and enhancing model performance. Practical code snippets and

As we wrap up, it is clear that the journey through classification-based

Chapter 4
Image Labeling Using Transfer
Learning
Introduction
Image labeling is a fundamental task in computer vision, enabling machines
to identify and categorize objects within images. Traditional methods often
require large datasets and extensive computational resources, making the
process time-consuming and resource-intensive. However, transfer learning
has emerged as a powerful technique to address these challenges.
Transfer learning involves leveraging pre-trained models that have already
learned to recognize patterns from vast amounts of data. By fine-tuning
these models for specific tasks, we can achieve high accuracy in image
labeling with smaller datasets and reduced training time. This approach not
only accelerates the development process but also makes advanced
machine learning (ML) techniques more accessible.
Structure
In this chapter, we will go through the following topics:
Transfer learning

--- Strana 136 ---
•
•
•
•
•
Data loading and preprocessing
Model training
Model evaluation and testing
Fine-tuning for transfer learning
Objectives
By the end of this chapter, readers will understand the basics of image
labeling and transfer learning. explore various transfer learning approaches,
applications, and benefits. And differentiate between types of transfer
learning. You will also learn about popular algorithms and their use in
transfer learning, understand the architecture of transfer learning models,
and prepare and process datasets effectively. Finally, you will learn how to
train and evaluate transfer learning models for image labeling tasks.
Transfer learning
Transfer learning is a ML technique where a model trained on one task is
applied to a different but related task. It leverages the knowledge acquired
during the training of the original task to improve learning on the new task.
Transfer learning is particularly useful when the new task has limited
labeled data or when training a model from scratch is computationally
expensive.
Transfer learning is a fundamental concept in ML and deep learning,
offering a powerful way to leverage existing models to solve new and
related problems efficiently. It has wide-ranging applications across various
domains and continues to advance the capabilities of ML models.
To understand the key components of transfer learning, it is essential to
distinguish between the source task, target task, and the process of fine-
tuning:
Source task: This is the original task on which a model is trained. It
provides 
a 
pre-trained 
model 
with 
learned 
features 
and

--- Strana 137 ---
•
•
1.
representations.
Target task: This is the new task for which you want to use the pre-
trained model. The target task may be related to the source task but
could have different objectives, data distributions, or labels.
Fine-tuning: Fine-tuning is adapting the pre-trained model for the
target task. It typically involves modifying the last few layers of the
model (output layers) while keeping the lower layers (feature
extraction layers) fixed.
The diagram below shows how transfer learning works:
Figure 4.1: Transfer learning
Working mechanism of transfer learning
In this section, we will learn the working mechanism of transfer learning:
Utilizing pre-trained models: A model like residual networks
(ResNet) is trained on ImageNet with over 14 million images across
1,000 different categories. ResNet-50 has 50 layers and contains
over 23 million parameters. These pre-trained models learn general
features like edges, textures, and object parts in the early layers,

--- Strana 138 ---
2.
3.
•
○
○
○
•
○
while the deeper layers capture more complex patterns. By using
transfer learning, we can leverage the learned features of a large,
diverse dataset without starting from scratch.
Fine-tuning for specific tasks: In transfer learning, the general
features from pre-trained models can be applied to more specific
tasks, like classifying medical images or species of animals, even if
the domain of images differs. Instead of retraining the entire model,
you typically only fine-tune the last few layers, adapting the model
to recognize domain-specific patterns.
Reduced data requirement: Training a deep model like ResNet
from scratch would require large datasets. However, with transfer
learning, smaller, task-specific datasets (such as a dataset of medical
images or animals) are enough. The pre-trained model already
knows how to extract useful features, and we only need to tweak it
for our use case.
When using transfer learning, there are two main approaches:
Feature extraction:
Here, the pre-trained model is used as a fixed feature extractor.
Only the last fully connected layer is replaced with a new one
tailored to the specific task (e.g., if classifying animals, you
replace the output layer with a layer that outputs the number of
animal classes).
In this case, the earlier layers remain frozen, meaning their
weights are not updated during training. This dramatically
reduces the number of updated parameters, leading to faster
training and less risk of overfitting on a small dataset.
Fine-tuning:
In this approach, some of the deeper layers are unfrozen and
retrained on the new dataset while keeping the earlier layers
frozen.

--- Strana 139 ---
○
○
•
•
•
•
•
Typically, the earlier layers (which capture low-level features
like edges and textures) are kept frozen, while deeper layers are
retrained to adapt to task-specific features.
For ResNet-50, only a fraction of the 23 million parameters
(such as the last few million) would be fine-tuned, rather than
all of them. This reduces the computational cost and the risk of
overfitting, especially when working with small datasets.
Applications of transfer learning
Transfer learning has a wide-range of applications across various domains
and fields. It is a versatile technique that allows pre-trained models to be
adapted to new tasks and domains. Some typical applications of transfer
learning include:
Image classification: Transfer learning is widely used in image
classification tasks. Pre-trained convolutional neural networks
(CNNs), such as Visual Geometry Group (VGG), and ResNet are
fine-tuned for specific image classification tasks.
Object detection: Transfer learning is applied to object detection
tasks, where models are adapted to detect and locate objects within
images or video frames. Models like Faster R-CNN, YOLO, and
SSD use transfer learning to enhance their detection capabilities.
Natural language processing (NLP): In NLP, pre-trained language
models like BERT, GPT etc. are fine-tuned for tasks such as
sentiment analysis, named entity recognition, machine translation,
and text summarization.
Speech recognition: Transfer learning is used in speech recognition
tasks, where models trained on a large corpus of speech data are
adapted for specific voice recognition, transcription, or voice
assistant applications.
Recommendation 
systems: 
Transfer 
learning 
improves
recommendation systems by leveraging user behavior data and pre-

--- Strana 140 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
trained embeddings to enhance personalized recommendations.
Medical imaging: Transfer learning aids in analyzing medical
images, including x-rays, MRIs, and CT scans, for tasks like disease
diagnosis, tumor detection, and anomaly identification.
Autonomous vehicles: Self-driving cars utilize transfer learning to
adapt pre-trained models for object detection, road segmentation,
and other perception tasks, making them safer and more efficient.
Video analysis: Transfer learning enhances video analysis tasks
such as action recognition, summarization, and captioning, allowing
models to understand video content.
Anomaly detection: Transfer learning is applied to anomaly
detection tasks, including cybersecurity, manufacturing, and finance,
to identify unusual patterns or behaviors.
Text-to-speech (TTS) synthesis: Transfer learning models generate
human-like speech from text input, improving the quality of TTS
synthesis systems.
Style transfer: In computer graphics, transfer learning is used for
style transfer tasks, allowing the artistic style of one image or
painting to be applied to another.
Robotics: Robots benefit from transfer learning to acquire new
skills and adapt to various environments, such as navigating object
manipulation and human-robot interaction.
Fraud detection: Financial institutions use it for fraud detection,
identifying fraudulent transactions by learning from historical data
patterns.
Healthcare: Transfer learning assists in healthcare tasks, including
disease prediction, patient risk assessment, and drug discovery, by
transferring knowledge from related medical domains.

•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Benefits of transfer learning
Transfer learning offers several significant benefits in the field of ML and
deep learning:
Improved learning efficiency: Transfer learning accelerates the
training process for new tasks by initializing models with pre-
trained weights. This saves time and computational resources.
Improved generalization: Pre-trained models have learned useful
features and representations from vast amounts of data, improving
generalization on target tasks with limited data.
Handling small datasets: Transfer learning is particularly valuable
when dealing with small datasets for the target task. It helps mitigate
overfitting and improves model performance.
Fewer data annotation costs: Reducing the need for extensive
labeled data collection is cost-effective. Transfer learning allows
models to leverage knowledge from publicly available pre-trained
models.
Adaptability: Transfer learning can be applied to various domains,
including image classification, object detection, sentiment analysis,
and more, making it versatile and adaptable.
Performance improvement: Transfer learning often leads to
improved performance on target tasks, particularly when the source
and target tasks are related. Fine-tuning a pre-trained model can lead
to 5% to 20% accuracy improvements on tasks compared to training
from scratch.
State-of-the-art results: Pre-trained models are often state-of-the-
art in their respective domains. Fine-tuning these models for specific
tasks can yield top-tier results.
Human-like learning: Transfer learning mirrors the human learning
process, where knowledge gained from one context is applied to

--- Strana 142 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
learning in other domains.
Fast prototyping: Researchers and practitioners can quickly
prototype and experiment with models for various tasks by
leveraging pre-trained models as a starting point.
Multimodal learning: Transfer learning can be applied to tasks that
involve multiple data modalities, such as images and text, enabling
the integration of information from different sources.
Continual learning: Transfer learning facilitates continual learning,
where models accumulate knowledge from various tasks over time,
building upon their capabilities.
Knowledge sharing: Transfer learning encourages the sharing of
pre-trained models and knowledge, fostering a collaborative and
open research community.
Types of transfer learning
Transfer learning can be categorized into various types based on the
relationships between the source and target tasks, the data availability, and
how the transfer of knowledge is performed. The main types of transfer
learning include:
Inductive transfer learning: In inductive transfer learning, the
source and target tasks are different and unrelated. The knowledge
learned from the source task is applied to the target task, often by
fine-tuning a pre-trained model or using its features.
Transductive transfer learning: Transductive transfer learning
deals with tasks that are related but not identical. In this case, the
model transfers knowledge from the source task to the target task
with some adaptation. Transductive transfer learning aims to
improve the performance of both the source and target tasks by
leveraging their commonalities.

--- Strana 143 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
Unsupervised transfer learning: Unsupervised transfer learning
involves pre-training a model on an unsupervised task and then fine-
tuning it for a supervised target task. Unsupervised pre-training is
often used for tasks where labeled data is scarce.
Semi-supervised transfer learning: Semi-supervised transfer
learning combines aspects of supervised and unsupervised transfer
learning. The model is pre-trained on a large dataset (unsupervised)
and then fine-tuned using a smaller amount of labeled data
(supervised) for the target task.
Multi-task learning (MTL): MTL is a form of transfer learning
where a single model is trained to perform multiple tasks
simultaneously. The model shares feature extraction layers but has
separate output layers for each task—this type of transfer learning
benefits from the joint learning of related tasks.
One-shot learning: One-shot learning is a type of transfer learning
where the model is trained to recognize or differentiate between
classes with very few examples (usually one or a few per class).
One-shot learning is particularly useful in scenarios with limited
data.
Zero-shot learning (ZSL): ZSL extends one-shot learning to
scenarios where the model must recognize classes that were not
present during training. It learns to generalize and recognize unseen
classes based on related attributes or semantic information.
Domain adaptation: Domain adaptation is a type of transfer
learning where the model is adapted from one domain (e.g., source
domain) to work effectively in another domain (e.g., target domain).
It is commonly used to address the domain shift problem when the
data distributions in the source and target domains differ.
Few-shot learning: Few-shot learning falls between one-shot
learning and traditional supervised learning. The model is trained to

--- Strana 144 ---
•
recognize classes with few examples, making it suitable for tasks
with limited data.
Meta-learning: Meta-learning, also known as learning to learn,
involves training a model to adapt quickly to new tasks. It is used in
situations where the model needs to be highly adaptable to a variety
of tasks.
Here is a table providing examples of where each type is used:

The choice of transfer learning type depends on the specific task, the
relationship between the source and target domains, the availability of
labeled data, and the desired level of adaptation between tasks. Each type of
transfer learning addresses different challenges and requirements in ML and
deep learning applications.
Algorithms used for transfer learning
Several ML algorithms and techniques are commonly used for transfer
learning:
Pre-trained language models: In NLP, pre-trained language models
like BERT, GPT, and RoBERTa are fine-tuned for various tasks,

--- Strana 146 ---
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
•
including sentiment analysis, text classification, question answering,
and more.
Residual transfer networks (RTN): RTN extends the idea of
ResNets and allows for knowledge transfer between related tasks.
Domain-adversarial neural networks (DANN): DANN is used for
domain adaptation, particularly when the source and target domains
have different distributions. It employs a domain classifier to align
the feature distributions of the two domains.
MTL: MTL involves training a single model to perform multiple
tasks simultaneously. The model shares feature extraction layers and
has separate output layers for each task.
Progressive neural networks: Progressive neural networks build
upon the idea of fine-tuning and involve training new tasks
incrementally. Models are trained for multiple tasks, each adding
new layers.
Transfer learning via learning to rank (LTR): LTR is used in
information retrieval tasks. It focuses on ranking the relevance of
items and transferring knowledge learned from ranking source data
to the target task.
Siamese networks: Siamese networks are used for similarity-based
tasks like face recognition and image verification. They learn to
measure the similarity or dissimilarity between pairs of inputs.
Meta-learning algorithms: Meta-learning algorithms aim to train
models that quickly adapt to new tasks with minimal data.
Approaches like model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML) are used
to enable fast adaptation.
ZSL and few-shot learning: Zero-shot and few-shot learning
algorithms transfer knowledge from a set of known classes to
recognize new or rare classes with minimal examples.

1.
2.
3.
4.

6.
a.
The choice of the transfer learning algorithm depends on the specific
problem, the available data, and the relationship between the source and
target tasks. Each algorithm is designed to handle different transfer learning
scenarios and challenges effectively.
Selecting the right transfer learning algorithm or approach involves
considering various factors related to your specific problem, data, and
available resources. Here is a step-by-step guide to help you choose the
most suitable transfer learning method:
Understand your problem: Start by gaining a deep understanding
of your target task. Define your problem, including the type of data
you have, the problem's complexity, and the desired outcome.
Analyze the data: Examine the size and quality of your dataset for
the target task. Consider the amount of labeled data available, data
distribution, and potential class imbalances.
Assess domain similarity: Evaluate the similarity between the
source and target domains. Consider whether the source task is
related to the target task and whether they share common features.
Determine your resources: Take into account the computational
resources, budget, and time constraints you have for the project.
Some transfer learning methods may be more computationally
intensive than others.
Consider pre-trained models: Explore pre-trained models and
datasets that are available in your problem domain. For tasks like
computer vision and NLP, pre-trained models are commonly used as
a starting point.
Choose the right transfer learning method: Experiment, evaluate,
iterate, and tune.
Implement the selected transfer learning method and conduct
experiments.

using

Based on the evaluation results, iterate and fine-tune the model
and method. Adjust hyperparameters, model architectures, and
data preprocessing as needed.
Architecture of transfer learning system
The architecture of transfer learning can vary depending on the specific
application and the choice of transfer learning technique. However, there
are common architectural elements and steps involved in the process. Here
is a typical architecture for transfer learning:
Source model: The source model is a pre-trained model trained on a
source 
task. 
This 
model 
has 
learned 
valuable 
features,
representations, and knowledge from the source task, making it a
starting point for transfer learning.
Feature extraction layers: The lower layers of the source model
(e.g., convolutional layers in a neural network) are often referred to
as feature extraction layers. These layers capture generic and
reusable features from the source task data. Sometimes, these layers
are frozen, meaning they are not updated during the transfer learning
process.
Task-specific layers: On top of the feature extraction layers, there
are task-specific layers. These layers are added or fine-tuned for the
target task. They are responsible for adapting the model to the new
task, making predictions, and capturing task-specific information.
Fine-tuning (Optional): Fine-tuning may be applied to the model
depending on the transfer learning approach. Fine-tuning involves
updating the task-specific layers to make them more suitable for the
target task. The extent of fine-tuning can vary, from updating only
the last layers to updating some or all.
New task data: The new task data consists of examples specific to
the target task. These examples are used to train and evaluate the

--- Strana 149 ---
•
•
•
•
•
○
○
model for the target task. It is essential to have sufficient labeled
data for the target task to ensure model performance.
Training process: The model is trained on the new task data using
an appropriate loss function and optimization algorithm. During
training, the model learns to make predictions and adjust its
parameters to minimize the loss on the target task.
Evaluation: After training, the model is evaluated on a separate
validation or test dataset for the target task. Evaluation metrics
relevant to the specific task assess the model's performance.
Inference: Once the model is trained and validated, it can make
predictions or inferences on new, unseen data for the target task. The
model's task-specific layers have been adapted to perform well on
this task.
Model deployment: The final trained model can be deployed for
practical applications, such as image classification, object detection,
text generation, or any other specific task the model was adapted for.
The architecture of transfer learning is flexible and can be adapted to
different scenarios and tasks. Depending on the complexity of the source
and target tasks, the architecture may involve more or fewer fine-tuning
steps, and the choice of layers to freeze or update can vary. The key is to
leverage the knowledge learned from the source task while effectively
adapting the model to the new target task.
Let us discuss the architectural details of ZSL and few-shot learning:
ZSL architecture:
Input layer: Features from unseen classes.
Feature extraction layer (Frozen): Pre-trained model layers
that extract general features from the input. These layers are
typically frozen in ZSL because the model relies on general
features learned from a vast amount of data during the training
on seen classes.

--- Strana 150 ---
○
○
•
○
Semantic embedding layer (Frozen): This maps the features
to a semantic space, often using attributes or word embeddings
like GloVe or Word2Vec to represent the unseen classes.
Classifier layer (Unfrozen): The classifier can be fine-tuned
for the ZSL task. Since no examples of unseen classes are
available, the classifier works based on the semantic
embeddings and the general feature mappings.
Flow of data:
The input (e.g., an image or text) first passes through the
feature extraction layer to generate high-level features. These
features are mapped into a semantic embedding space where
unseen and seen classes share a semantic relationship. Finally,
the data reaches the classifier, which uses the semantic
representation to classify the input into one of the unseen
classes.
This architecture allows the model to classify new categories based solely
on the semantic relationships learned between seen and unseen classes
rather than needing direct examples of the unseen classes.
The diagram below shows the overall architecture:
Figure 4.2: Transfer learning architecture

•

Popular datasets for transfer learning
Here are some popular datasets that can help you understand and practice
transfer learning:
ImageNet: ImageNet is one of the most well-known image
classification datasets. It contains millions of labeled images across
thousands of categories. It is often used as a source dataset for pre-
training models in transfer learning.
URL: https://image-net.org/download.php
CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100: They are popular image classification
datasets containing smaller sets of images (10 and 100 classes,
respectively). These datasets are commonly used for fine-tuning pre-
trained models.
URL: https://www.cs.toronto.edu/~kriz/cifar.html
MNIST: It is a dataset of handwritten digits. While it is not as
challenging as some other datasets, it is helpful in practicing transfer
learning concepts in the context of image classification.
URL: http://yann.lecun.com/exdb/mnist/
IMDB Movie Reviews: The IMDB dataset contains movie reviews
labeled as positive or negative sentiment. It is often used for transfer
learning in natural language tasks like sentiment analysis.
URL: https://ai.stanford.edu/~amaas/data/sentiment/
Wikipedia text: Wikipedia provides a large corpus of text in
multiple languages. It can be used for pre-training language models
in NLP tasks.
URL: https://dumps.wikimedia.org/
Stanford Dogs and Cats: This dataset contains images of dogs and
cats and is used for fine-tuning pre-trained models for binary image
classification.

--- Strana 152 ---
•
•
URL: http://vision.stanford.edu/aditya86/ImageNetDogs/
URL: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/download/details.aspx?
id=54765
Oxford Flowers and Oxford 102 Birds: These datasets contain
images of flowers and birds, respectively. They are used for fine-
tuning models in tasks related to fine-grained image classification.
URL: http://www.robots.ox.ac.uk/~vgg/data/flowers/
URL: http://www.robots.ox.ac.uk/~vgg/data/flowers/102/
Healthcare datasets: Datasets related to medical imaging, such as
Chest X-rays, MRI scans, or pathology slides, are often used for
transfer learning in healthcare applications like disease detection
and diagnosis.
URL: https://www.kaggle.com/nih-chest-xrays/data
When working with these datasets, you can follow common transfer
learning workflows, such as pre-training a model on a source task (using a
large dataset like ImageNet) and fine-tuning it for a target task (using a
smaller, task-specific dataset). These datasets will help you grasp the
fundamental concepts of transfer learning, model adaptation, and
knowledge transfer across different domains and tasks.
Data loading and preprocessing
This code demonstrates a basic setup in PyTorch for loading and visualizing
a subset of the CIFAR-10 dataset. It starts by importing necessary libraries
such as torch, torchvision, and matplotlib.pyplot. Then, it defines the
computing device, which will use a GPU if available, otherwise it defaults
to the CPU.
The transform variable specifies data transformations, resizing images to
32x32 pixels, converting them to tensors, and normalizing them. The

--- Strana 153 ---
CIFAR-10 dataset is loaded and split into training and testing sets, with the
trainset and testset representing the two groups.
To speed up training, a random subset of 1,000 images is selected from the
training data. DataLoader are created for both training and testing,
allowing efficient data batch handling.
The imshow function visualizes an unnormalized image. The code retrieves
a batch of images and displays them in a grid, giving a preview of the
CIFAR-10 images.
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.optim as optim
import torchvision
import torchvision.transforms as transforms
import random
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
import torch.nn.functional as F # Import functional API for activation
functions
# Define the device
device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
# Define data transformations
transform = transforms.Compose([
